\title{\textbf{Space--Time Algebra}\\[0.35em]
  \large A compact development of paravectors and their physical applications}
\author{Dr. Luke A. Wendt}
\date{}

\begin{document}
\pagenumbering{roman}
\maketitle
\thispagestyle{empty}

\begin{abstract}
A complex scalar and three-vector form a paravector algebra combining dot
and cross products. It expresses quaternions, rotations, Lorentz boosts,
projections, spectral functions, spacetime geometry, relativistic dynamics,
electromagnetism, gauge coupling, Klein--Gordon and Dirac equations,
Schr\"odinger and Pauli limits, and spin amplitudes and probabilities.
Shared definitions connect these results through short derivations:
spacetime determinants give proper time, four-momentum, and the mass shell;
energy and momentum operators yield Klein--Gordon, whose first-order
factorization gives Dirac. Spin one-half emerges naturally from the
rotation structure of paravector spinors. The same products and projectors
give its magnetic coupling and measurement probabilities.
\end{abstract}

\clearpage
\begingroup
\setlength{\parskip}{0pt}
\tableofcontents
\endgroup
\clearpage
\pagenumbering{arabic}

\section{Algebra and paravectors}
\label{sec:algebra}

\subsection{Notation and physical conventions}

We use natural units $\hbar=c=1$, rationalized electromagnetic units,
and signature $(+,-,-,-)$. Bold symbols denote vectors, ordered generator
triples, or, where specified, matrices.

Latin paravectors and paravector operators use bold calligraphic capitals.
Greek paravector symbols retain their usual glyphs, with paravector-valued
$\pvpsi$, $\pvphi$, and $\pvvarphi$ in bold. The unsubscripted
paravector derivative $\pvpartial$ is also bold; derivatives such as
$\partial_t$ and $\pdr$ use an unbolded partial symbol and bold vector subscripts. Scalar quantities
and coefficient components remain unadorned. Vectors and matrices remain bold.
These styles distinguish, for example, a boost $\pL$ from a scalar
Lagrangian $L$, and the paravector momentum $\pP$ from its vector part $\bp$.


We use the algebra of physical space (APS), $\mathrm{Cl}_{3,0}$,
isomorphic to the even subalgebra of spacetime algebra,
$\mathrm{Cl}_{1,3}^{+}$~\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~1--2}{page=1}.

We identify $c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ with its coefficient pair
$(c,\bZ)\in\C\oplus\C^3$. Real paravectors lie in $\R\oplus\R^3$;
mixed sums specify the two coefficient spaces separately.
The direct sum describes values, while the sigma rules define multiplication.
The generators $\pvsigma_k$ are algebra elements; $\bsig$ is their ordered
triple, not a numerical vector in $\R^3$.

Domains persist within a section unless a specialization is stated.
Primes and subscripts inherit the declared type unless explicitly redefined.
For fields, membership states the value at each real spacetime point;
function notation specifies the independent variables.
All differentiations assume the required regularity.

\subsection{Sigma generators and ordered multiplication}

The generators $\pvsigma_n$, $n=1,2,3$, obey ordered multiplication rules.
Their products generally do not commute.  The panel gives their defining
relations and the general complex element~\citeat{baylis2004}{pp.~4--5}{page=4}:

\begin{center}
\setlength{\fboxsep}{1.2em}
\fcolorbox{black}{white}{%
\begin{minipage}{0.88\textwidth}
\begin{align*}
 \pvsigma_n^2&=1,
 &\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m&=-\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n\quad(n\ne m),\\
 \ii&=\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3,
 &\ii^2&=-1,\\
 \bsig&=\{\pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_2,\pvsigma_3\},
 &\ii\bsig&=\{\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3,\pvsigma_3\pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\},\\
 \pZ&=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\bY\ii)\cdot\bsig,
 &a,b&\in\R,\quad \bX,\bY\in\R^3.
\end{align*}
\end{minipage}}
\end{center}

Ordinary three-vector operations use
\begin{align*}
 \bX\cdot\bY&=X_1Y_1+X_2Y_2+X_3Y_3,\\
 \bX\times\bY&=\{X_2Y_3-X_3Y_2,
 X_3Y_1-X_1Y_3,X_1Y_2-X_2Y_1\},\\
 \bX^2&:=\bX\cdot\bX.
\end{align*}
Thus the square of a vector always denotes its self dot product.
For complex vectors, dot and cross products use the same formulas, without
conjugating either factor.
The elementary Cartesian vectors used below are the real vectors
\[
 \bm e_1=\{1,0,0\},\qquad
 \bm e_2=\{0,1,0\},\qquad
 \bm e_3=\{0,0,1\}.
\]
Thus $\bm e_k\cdot\bsig=\pvsigma_k$; the scalar basis element is $1$.
Unindexed $e$ denotes only Euler's number.

These eight elements form a complete basis, displayed by type as
\[
 \underbrace{1}_{\text{scalar}}\qquad
 \underbrace{\pvsigma_1,\pvsigma_2,\pvsigma_3}_{\text{vectors}}\qquad
 \underbrace{\ii\pvsigma_1,\ii\pvsigma_2,\ii\pvsigma_3}_{\text{bivectors}}\qquad
 \underbrace{\ii}_{\text{pseudoscalar}}.
\]
Anticommutation gives
$\ii^2=-\pvsigma_1^2\pvsigma_2^2\pvsigma_3^2=-1$.  The pseudoscalar is central:
moving $\pvsigma_n$ through the other two generators produces two sign
changes, so
\begin{equation*}
 \ii\pvsigma_n=\pvsigma_n\ii.
\end{equation*}
Thus its order of multiplication does not matter.

For $\pX,\pY\in\C\oplus\C^3$, the commutator is
\begin{gather*}
 [\pX,\pY]:=\pX \pY-\pY \pX,\\
 [\pY,\pX]=-[\pX,\pY].
\end{gather*}
Thus $[\pX,\pY]=0$ exactly when $\pX$ and $\pY$ commute.
For $n,m,k=1,2,3$, define the Kronecker delta by
\begin{equation*}
 \delta_{nm}:=\begin{aligned}[t]
  &1\quad\text{if }n=m,\\
  &0\quad\text{if }n\ne m,
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
and the Levi--Civita symbol by
\begin{equation*}
 \epsilon_{nmk}:=\begin{aligned}[t]
  &+1\quad\text{if }(n,m,k)\text{ is an even permutation of }(1,2,3),\\
  &-1\quad\text{if }(n,m,k)\text{ is an odd permutation of }(1,2,3),\\
  &\phantom{+}0\quad\text{if two or more indices are equal}.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
For $n,m=1,2,3$, the ordered multiplication rule and commutator are~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(3)}{page=4}\,\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(10)}{page=5}
\begin{align}
  \pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m=\delta_{nm}
  +\sum_{k=1}^{3}\epsilon_{nmk}\,\ii\pvsigma_k,
 \label{eq:sigma-product}\\
  [\pvsigma_n,\pvsigma_m]
  =2\sum_{k=1}^{3}\epsilon_{nmk}\,\ii\pvsigma_k. \notag
\end{align}
\subsection{Hermitian and sigma conjugation}
\label{sec:conjugate-operators}

Define \emph{Hermitian conjugation} (reversion in APS) by
\eqref{eq:hermitian-definition} and \emph{sigma conjugation} (grade involution)
by \eqref{eq:sigma-conjugation-definition}~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(3)}{page=2}:
\begin{align}
 (\,\cdot\,)^{\mathsf H}
 &:=\text{reverse the order of every product of sigma generators},
 \label{eq:hermitian-definition}\\
 (\,\cdot\,)^*
 &:=\text{replace every $\pvsigma_n$ by $-\pvsigma_n$ without changing product order}.
 \label{eq:sigma-conjugation-definition}
\end{align}
Thus, for two generators,
\begin{align*}
 (\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)^{\mathsf H}&=\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m,\\
 (\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)^*&=(-\pvsigma_m)(-\pvsigma_n).
\end{align*}
Both operations act term by term on sums and leave real coefficients fixed.
Since $\ii=\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3$, sigma conjugation preserves product
order and reverses each generator:
\[
 \ii^*=(-\pvsigma_1)(-\pvsigma_2)(-\pvsigma_3)
 =-\pvsigma_1\pvsigma_2\pvsigma_3=-\ii.
\]
It therefore complex conjugates the scalar coefficients while reversing
the sign of $\bsig$. Their actions on the four basis types are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c c c c c}
  \toprule
  type & scalar & vector & bivector & pseudoscalar\\
  representative & $1$ & $\pvsigma_n$ & $\ii\pvsigma_n$ & $\ii$\\
  \midrule
  $(\cdot)^*$ & $+$ & $-$ & $+$ & $-$\\
  $(\cdot)^{\mathsf H}$ & $+$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$\\
  $(\cdot)^{*\mathsf H}$ & $+$ & $-$ & $-$ & $+$\\
  \bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For example, on a bivector the signs follow directly:
\begin{align*}
 (\ii\pvsigma_n)^*&=\ii^*\pvsigma_n^*=\ii\pvsigma_n,\\
 (\ii\pvsigma_n)^{\mathsf H}
 &=\pvsigma_n^{\mathsf H}\ii^{\mathsf H}=-\ii\pvsigma_n.
\end{align*}
For $\pZ,\pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$, the definitions give the product
laws~\citeat{baylis2004}{p.~4}{page=4}\,\citeat{chappell-functions}{p.~2}{page=2}
\begin{align}
 (\pZ \pZ')^{\mathsf H}&=\pZ'^{\mathsf H}\pZ^{\mathsf H},
 \label{eq:hermitian-product}\\
 (\pZ \pZ')^*&=\pZ^*\pZ'^*.
 \label{eq:sigma-conjugate-product}
\end{align}
The operations commute with each other: $\pZ^{*\mathsf H}=\pZ^{\mathsf H*}$.
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix} gives both operations in the Pauli-matrix
representation, where $^{\mathsf H}$ is the usual matrix Hermitian conjugation.

\subsection{Complex paravectors and their components}
\label{sec:paravectors}

Let $a,b\in\R$ and $\bX,\bY\in\R^3$. The two real paravectors
$\pX,\pY\in\R\oplus\R^3$ combine into the general complex paravector
$\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \pX:&=a+\bX\cdot\bsig,\\
 \pY:&=b+\bY\cdot\bsig,\\[4pt]
 \pZ&=\pX+\ii \pY\\
  &=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig\\
  &=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Here $c=a+b\ii\in\C$ and $\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY\in\C^3$ are its
complex scalar and vector coefficients.

Thus $\operatorname{sc},\det,\operatorname{tr}$ take values in $\C$,
$\operatorname{vec}$ in $\C^3$, and $\operatorname{re},\operatorname{im}$
in $\R\oplus\R^3$. Conjugates, adjugates, and existing inverses take values
in $\C\oplus\C^3$.
Define the complex scalar and vector extractions by
\begin{align*}
 \scpart {\pZ}&:=c=a+\ii b,\\
 \vecpart {\pZ}&:=\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY.
\end{align*}
The complex scalar part combines scalar and pseudoscalar grades; the
complex vector term combines vector and bivector grades. This notation
applies throughout the algebra unless a real specialization is stated.

\subsection{Conjugates, determinant, and inverse}
\label{sec:algebra-operations}

On the coefficients, both conjugations give
$c^*=c^{\mathsf H}=a-b\ii$ and
$\bZ^*=\bZ^{\mathsf H}=\bX-\ii\bY$; on the paravector, the two
conjugations differ in the vector sign~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(45)}{page=11}:
\begin{align}
 \pZ^*&=c^*-\bZ^*\cdot\bsig
 =(a-b\ii)+(-\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:algebra-star}\\
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}&=c^*+\bZ^*\cdot\bsig
 =(a-b\ii)+(\bX-\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:algebra-H}
\end{align}
Their composition defines the adjugate~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(39)}{page=10}
\begin{equation}
 \adj {\pZ}:=\pZ^{*\mathsf H}=c-\bZ\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:adjugate}
\end{equation}
The trace is twice the scalar part:
\begin{equation}
 \operatorname{tr}(\pZ):=\pZ+\adj {\pZ}=2\scpart {\pZ}=2c.
 \label{eq:trace-scalar}
\end{equation}
The determinant is the complex scalar~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~104}{page=115}
\begin{equation}
 \det(\pZ):=\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ=\pZ\adj {\pZ}=c^2-\bZ^2.
 \label{eq:det}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$, adjugation reverses product order, giving determinant
multiplicativity:
\begin{align*}
 \adj{\pZ \pZ'}&=\adj{\pZ'}\adj {\pZ},
 \\
 \det(\pZ \pZ')&=\adj{\pZ'}(\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ)\pZ'
           =\det(\pZ)\det(\pZ').
\end{align*}
Since the determinants are scalars, the determinant of any product is
unchanged by reordering its factors.  For a scalar $z\in\C$,
\begin{align}
 \adj{z\pZ}&=z\adj {\pZ}, \notag\\
 \det(z\pZ)&=z^2\det(\pZ).
 \label{eq:det-scalar-scaling}
\end{align}
When $\det(\pZ)\ne0$, the inverse is~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(47)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^{-1}=\frac{\adj {\pZ}}{\det(\pZ)}
       =\frac{c-\bZ\cdot\bsig}{c^2-\bZ^2}.
 \label{eq:inverse}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ'=c'+\bZ'\cdot\bsig$, with $c'\in\C$, $\bZ'\in\C^3$, division is
order-sensitive.  An unambiguous quotient exists when $\pZ$ is invertible
and the factors commute:
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\pZ'}{\pZ}:=\pZ'\pZ^{-1}=\pZ^{-1}\pZ'
 \qquad\text{if }\bZ\times\bZ'=0.
\end{equation*}
Differentiating $\pZ^{-1}\pZ=1$ gives the inverse rule
\begin{equation*}
 \dd(\pZ^{-1})=-\pZ^{-1}(\dd \pZ)\pZ^{-1}.
\end{equation*}
For $\pZ,\pZ',\pZ''\in\C\oplus\C^3$, scalar part obeys the cyclic laws
\begin{align}
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=\scpart{\pZ'\pZ},
 \notag\\
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'\pZ''}&=\scpart{\pZ'\pZ''\pZ}=\scpart{\pZ''\pZ \pZ'}.
 \label{eq:scalar-cyclic-three}
\end{align}
Multiplying these identities by $2$ gives trace cyclicity.  Their similarity
invariants are derived in Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}.

\subsection{Real and imaginary extractions}

The real and imaginary extractions use Hermitian conjugation~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(9)}{page=5}:
\begin{align}
 \repart {\pZ}&:=\tfrac12(\pZ+\pZ^{\mathsf H})=\pX,
 \label{eq:algebra-re}\\
 \impart {\pZ}&:=\frac{\pZ-\pZ^{\mathsf H}}{2\ii}=\pY.
 \label{eq:algebra-im}
\end{align}
Adjugation evaluates the scalar and vector extractions explicitly~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(14)}{page=6}:
\begin{align*}
 \scpart {\pZ}&=\tfrac12(\pZ+\adj {\pZ})=c,
 \\
 (\vecpart {\pZ})\cdot\bsig&=\tfrac12(\pZ-\adj {\pZ})=\bZ\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
The functions $\operatorname{re}$ and $\operatorname{im}$ also act on
complex coefficients.  Composing extractions recovers all four real parts:
\begin{align*}
 a&=\repart c=\scpart{\repart {\pZ}},\\
 b&=\impart c=\scpart{\impart {\pZ}},\\
 \bX&=\repart{\bZ}=\vecpart{\repart {\pZ}},\\
 \bY&=\impart{\bZ}=\vecpart{\impart {\pZ}}.
\end{align*}
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix} reproduces these intrinsic operations with Pauli
matrices.

\section{Products and normalizations}
\label{sec:paravector-products}

\subsection{Invertibility and normalization}
\label{sec:unit-forms}

Recall $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$, with $c\in\C$ and $\bZ\in\C^3$.
For $\bZ\in\C^3$, distinguish the bilinear square from the squared norm:
\begin{align*}
 \bZ^2&:=\bZ\cdot\bZ,\\
 \lVert\bZ\rVert^2&:=\bZ\cdot\bZ^*.
\end{align*}
For $c\in\C$, set $\lVert c\rVert^2:=cc^*$.  The squared paravector norm is~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(46)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2:=\lVert c\rVert^2+\lVert\bZ\rVert^2
 =cc^*+\bZ\cdot\bZ^*.
 \label{eq:coefficient-norm}
\end{equation}
All norms take values in $\R_{\ge0}$ and denote nonnegative real square roots.
Here $\bu\in\C^3$ and $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
Each name below specifies a condition; a letter alone implies none.

\Needspace{7\baselineskip}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Vector of square one.} $\bu$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \bu^2=\bu\cdot\bu=1.
 \label{eq:bilinear-unit-vector}
\end{equation}
For $\bZ^2\ne0$, take $\bu=\bZ/\sqrt{\bZ^2}$ with either square root.
Its norm need not be one.  A nonzero vector with $\bZ^2=0$
cannot be normalized this way.
\item \textbf{Vector of unit norm.} $\bu$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\bu\rVert^2=\bu\cdot\bu^*=1.
\end{equation*}
For any $\bZ\ne0$, take $\bu=\bZ/\lVert\bZ\rVert$; then
$\bu^2=\bZ^2/\lVert\bZ\rVert^2$ need not be one.
\phantomsection\label{def:real-unit-vector}
For real vectors the two conditions coincide: a \emph{real unit vector}
has $\bu^2=\lVert\bu\rVert^2=1$.  For complex vectors, specify which holds.
\Needspace{14\baselineskip}
\item \textbf{Invertible paravector.} $\pZ$ has an inverse on both sides:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ \pZ^{-1}=\pZ^{-1}\pZ=1.
 \label{eq:unit-paravector}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $\det(\pZ)\ne0$. In algebra, an invertible element is
also called an \emph{algebraic unit}. This terminology does not impose
unit norm or determinant one; we use \emph{invertible} here to avoid
confusion with those normalization conditions.
\item \textbf{Unitary paravector.} Its Hermitian conjugate is its inverse:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ=1.
 \label{eq:unitary-transform}
\end{equation}
Then $\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ^{-1}$ and $\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}=1$.
\item \textbf{Paravector of determinant one.} $\pZ$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \det(\pZ)=1.
 \label{eq:unit-determinant-paravector}
\end{equation}
It is invertible with $\pZ^{-1}=\adj {\pZ}$; it need not be unitary.
For real $\pZ$, $\adj {\pZ}=\pZ^*$, so this condition is also $\pZ^*\pZ=1$.
This equivalence need not hold for complex $\pZ$.
\item \textbf{Paravector of unit norm.} $\pZ$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2=1.
 \label{eq:unit-norm-paravector}
\end{equation}
For $\pZ\ne0$, $\pZ'=\pZ/\lVert \pZ\rVert$ has unit norm.
It need not have an inverse: $(1+\bu\cdot\bsig)/\sqrt2$ has norm one
and determinant zero for any real unit vector $\bu$.
Unitarity implies unit norm by \eqref{eq:hermitian-norm}.
\item \textbf{Self-inverse paravector.} $\pZ$ is its own inverse:
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^2=1.
 \label{eq:involutory-paravector}
\end{equation}
Then $\pZ^{-1}=\pZ$; it is unitary exactly when $\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ$.
Its parts obey $c^2+\bZ^2=1$ and $2c\bZ=0$:
thus $\pZ=\pm1$ or $\pZ=\bu\cdot\bsig$ with $\bu^2=1$.
\end{itemize}

\Needspace{9\baselineskip}
Two complex vectors illustrate why the normalizations differ:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ccc}
\toprule
$\bZ$ & $\bZ^2$ & $\lVert\bZ\rVert^2$\\
\midrule
$\{\sqrt2,\ii,0\}$ & $1$ & $3$\\
$\{1,\ii,0\}$ & $0$ & $2$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Their vectors of unit norm have squares $1/3$ and $0$, respectively.
The physical roles of the paravector conditions are compared in
Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}.

\subsection{Products and quadratic forms}

For $\bX,\bY\in\C^3$, multiplication combines the dot and cross products;
both products are complex bilinear, with no conjugation.
The sigma rule~\eqref{eq:sigma-product} gives the dot--cross identity~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(38)}{page=10}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 (\bX\cdot\bsig)(\bY\cdot\bsig)
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}
   X_nY_m\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m\\
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}X_nY_m\delta_{nm}
   +\sum_{n=1}^{3}\sum_{m=1}^{3}\sum_{k=1}^{3}
   X_nY_m\epsilon_{nmk}\ii\pvsigma_k\\
 &=\bX\cdot\bY+\ii(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:vector-product}
\end{equation}
For real $\bX,\bY$, the product lies in $\R\oplus\ii\R^3$.
Adding the product with $\bX$ and $\bY$ interchanged gives
\begin{equation*}
 2(\bX\cdot\bY)
 =(\bX\cdot\bsig)(\bY\cdot\bsig)
 +(\bY\cdot\bsig)(\bX\cdot\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Consequently, for every nonnegative integer $n$,
\begin{align*}
 (\bX\cdot\bsig)^2&=\bX^2,\\
 (\bX\cdot\bsig)^{2n}&=(\bX^2)^n,\\
 (\bX\cdot\bsig)^{2n+1}&=(\bX^2)^n\bX\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Also,
\begin{equation*}
 (\bX\cdot\bsig)\bsig=\bX-\ii\bX\times\bsig.
\end{equation*}
Subtracting the two orders gives
\begin{equation*}
 [\bX\cdot\bsig,\bY\cdot\bsig]
 =2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig.
\end{equation*}
Hence
\begin{align*}
 [\bX\cdot\bsig,\bsig]&=-2\ii\bX\times\bsig,\\
 [\bX\cdot\ii\bsig,\bsig]&=2\bX\times\bsig,\\
 [\bX\cdot\bsig,\bsig]\cdot\bY
 &=2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Real products}

For real $\pX=a+\bX\cdot\bsig$ and $\pY=b+\bY\cdot\bsig$, with
$a,b\in\R$ and $\bX,\bY\in\R^3$, \eqref{eq:vector-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 \pX \pY&=(ab+\bX\cdot\bY)
 +(a\bY+b\bX+\ii\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig,
 \\
 [\pX,\pY]&=2\ii(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig, \\
 \pX^2&=(a^2+\bX^2)+2a\bX\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Thus $\pX \pY\in\R\oplus\C^3$, $[\pX,\pY]\in\{0\}\oplus\ii\R^3$, and
$\pX^2\in\R\oplus\R^3$. Real paravectors are not closed under multiplication.
The real quadratic identity is \eqref{eq:minimal} with $c=a$.
If $\bX\times\bY=0$, then $\pX \pY=\pY \pX$; when $\det(\pX)\ne0$, also
$\pX^{-1}\pY=\pY \pX^{-1}$.

\subsection{Complex products}

Let $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ and $\pZ'=c'+\bZ'\cdot\bsig$, where
$c,c'\in\C$ and $\bZ,\bZ'\in\C^3$.  Complex bilinearity extends
\eqref{eq:vector-product} to the general product
\begin{equation}
 \pZ \pZ'=(cc'+\bZ\cdot\bZ')+
 \bigl(c\bZ'+c'\bZ+\ii\bZ\times\bZ'\bigr)\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:para-product}
\end{equation}

The result $\pZ \pZ'\in\C\oplus\C^3$ has a complex scalar and complex vector part.
Thus
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=cc'+\bZ\cdot\bZ',\\
 \vecpart{\pZ \pZ'}&=c\bZ'+c'\bZ+\ii\bZ\times\bZ'.
\end{align*}
Equivalently, write $\pZ=\pX+\ii \pY$ and $\pZ'=\pX'+\ii \pY'$ with all four
factors real.  Their products need not be real, but distributivity gives
\begin{equation*}
 \pZ \pZ'=(\pX \pX'-\pY \pY')+\ii(\pX \pY'+\pY \pX').
\end{equation*}

Write $\pZ=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig$ with real
$a,b,\bX,\bY$, and use primes for the second paravector's real parts.  The real and imaginary
scalar and vector parts of $\pZ \pZ'$ are
\begin{align*}
 \text{real scalar: }&aa'-bb'+\bX\cdot\bX'-\bY\cdot\bY',\\
 \text{imaginary scalar: }&ab'+ba'+\bX\cdot\bY'+\bY\cdot\bX',\\
 \text{real vector: }&a\bX'-b\bY'+a'\bX-b'\bY
                    -\bX\times\bY'-\bY\times\bX',\\
 \text{imaginary vector: }&a\bY'+b\bX'+a'\bY+b'\bX
                    +\bX\times\bX'-\bY\times\bY'.
\end{align*}
Specializing \eqref{eq:para-product} gives the quadratic products:
\begin{align}
 \pZ^2&=(a^2-b^2+\bX^2-\bY^2)
 +2\ii(ab+\bX\cdot\bY) \notag\\
 &\quad+2(a\bX-b\bY)\cdot\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY+b\bX)\cdot\bsig, \notag\\
 \pZ \pZ^*&=(a^2+b^2-\bX^2-\bY^2)
 -2(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY-b\bX)\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:star-product-left}\\
 \pZ^*\pZ&=(a^2+b^2-\bX^2-\bY^2)
 +2(\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig
 +2\ii(a\bY-b\bX)\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:star-product-right}\\
 \pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}&=a^2+b^2+\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2(a\bX+b\bY+\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:hermitian-product-left}\\
 \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ&=a^2+b^2+\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2(a\bX+b\bY-\bX\times\bY)\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:hermitian-product-right}\\
 \pZ \pZ^{*\mathsf H}&=\det(\pZ)
 =a^2-b^2-\bX^2+\bY^2
 +2\ii(ab-\bX\cdot\bY). \notag
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:star-product-left} and \eqref{eq:star-product-right}
have values in $\R\oplus\C^3$ and show $\pZ \pZ^*=\pZ^*\pZ$ exactly when
$\bX\times\bY=0$.  Equations
\eqref{eq:hermitian-product-left} and \eqref{eq:hermitian-product-right}
take values in $\R\oplus\R^3$ and give the same condition for
$\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ$.

\subsection{Norm and determinant}
For the complex coefficients $c=a+b\ii$ and $\bZ=\bX+\ii\bY$, the squared norms
defined above are
\begin{align*}
 \lVert c\rVert^2&=a^2+b^2,\\
 \lVert\bZ\rVert^2&=\bX^2+\bY^2.
\end{align*}
For the squared norm~\eqref{eq:coefficient-norm}, the quadratic products
and cyclicity give the Hermitian form~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(46)}{page=11}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lVert \pZ\rVert^2&=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}
 =\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}(\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ)
 =\scpart{\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\scpart{\pZ^*\pZ^{*\mathsf H}}
 =\scpart{\pZ^{*\mathsf H}\pZ^*}.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:hermitian-norm}
\end{equation}
For complex coefficients, $c^2$ and $\bZ^2$ generally differ from these
squared norms.  Also $\det(\pZ)$ may be complex.  For a complex scalar
$z=r e^{\ii\theta}$, with real $r\ge0$ and $\theta$, scalar scaling gives
\begin{align}
 \det(z\pZ)&=r^2e^{2\ii\theta}\det(\pZ), \notag\\
 \lVert z\pZ\rVert^2&=\lVert z\rVert^2\lVert \pZ\rVert^2
 =r^2\lVert \pZ\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:norm-scalar-scaling}
\end{align}
The determinant retains the scalar phase by \eqref{eq:det-scalar-scaling};
the norm loses it by \eqref{eq:norm-scalar-scaling}.

\section{Projectors and spectral functions}
\label{sec:spectral}

\subsection{Quadratic identity and eigenvalues}

Recall $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$, with $c\in\C$ and $\bZ\in\C^3$.
Since $\pZ-c=\bZ\cdot\bsig$ and
$(\bZ\cdot\bsig)^2=\bZ^2$,
\begin{align*}
 (\pZ-c)^2&=\bZ^2,\\
 \pZ^2-2c\pZ+(c^2-\bZ^2)&=0.
\end{align*}
Using $\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)=2c$ and
$\det(\pZ)=c^2-\bZ^2$ gives the trace--determinant identity
\begin{equation}
 \pZ^2-\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)\pZ+\det(\pZ)=0.
  \label{eq:minimal}
\end{equation}
An eigenvalue is a scalar $\lambda\in\C$ for which $\pZ-\lambda$ has no
inverse.  Thus $\det(\pZ-\lambda)=\lambda^2-2c\lambda+\det(\pZ)=0$,
the scalar quadratic corresponding to \eqref{eq:minimal}.  Its roots are
\begin{equation*}
 \lambda_\pm
 =\frac{\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)
 \pm\sqrt{\operatorname{tr}(\pZ)^2-4\det(\pZ)}}{2}
 =c\pm\sqrt{\bZ^2}.
\end{equation*}
Because $\bZ$ is generally complex, $\bZ^2=\bZ\cdot\bZ$ and its square
root may also be complex.
The quadratic identity therefore factors as
\begin{equation*}
 (\pZ-\lambda_-)(\pZ-\lambda_+)=0.
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Projection operators}
\label{sec:algebra-projectors}

For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, the
paravector $\bu\cdot\bsig\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$ is Hermitian and
\hyperref[eq:involutory-paravector]{self-inverse}.  Define its two orthogonal projectors~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~121}{page=132}
\begin{equation}
 \pvPi_\pm:=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig).
 \label{eq:projectors}
\end{equation}

Here $\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\R^3$ with scalar part $1/2$.
Since $(\bu\cdot\bsig)^2=1$,
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm^2&=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_+\pvPi_-&=0,\\
 \pvPi_++\pvPi_-&=1,\\
 \pvPi_\pm^{\mathsf H}&=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_\pm^*&=\pvPi_\mp,\\
 (\bu\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\pm&=\pm \pvPi_\pm.
\end{align*}
Thus $\bu\cdot\bsig=\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-$.  For a second
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu'$,
\begin{align*}
 (\bu\cdot\bsig)(\bu'\cdot\bsig)
 &=\bu\cdot\bu'+\ii(\bu\times\bu')\cdot\bsig,\\
 [\bu\cdot\bsig,\bu'\cdot\bsig]
 &=2\ii(\bu\times\bu')\cdot\bsig,\\
 (\bu\cdot\bsig)(\bu'\cdot\bsig)(\bu\cdot\bsig)
 &=\left(2(\bu\cdot\bu')\bu-\bu'\right)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Define $\bu'_{\parallel}=(\bu'\cdot\bu)\bu$ and
$\bu'_{\perp}=\bu'-\bu'_{\parallel}$, both in $\R^3$.  Then
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm(\bu'\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\pm
 &=\pm(\bu\cdot\bu')\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_\pm(\bu'\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\mp
 &=\tfrac12(\bu'_{\perp}\pm\ii(\bu\times\bu'))\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
For $\pvPi_\pm'=(1\pm\bu'\cdot\bsig)/2\in\R\oplus\R^3$, all four products lie in $\R\oplus\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm\pvPi_\pm'&=\tfrac14(1+\bu\cdot\bu')
 +\tfrac14(\pm\bu\pm\bu'+\ii\bu\times\bu')\cdot\bsig,\\
 \pvPi_\pm\pvPi_\mp'&=\tfrac14(1-\bu\cdot\bu')
 +\tfrac14(\pm\bu\mp\bu'-\ii\bu\times\bu')\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Spectral rule}
Let $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$, with $c\in\C$, $\bZ\in\C^3$, and
$\bZ^2\ne0$.  Define the complex scalars $w,\lambda_\pm$, complex vector $\bu$, and
projectors $\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\C^3$ by
\begin{align*}
 w&:=+\sqrt{\bZ^2},& z&:=w,\\
 \bu&:=\bZ/w,& \pZ&=c+z\bu\cdot\bsig,\\
 \lambda_\pm&:=c\pm z,& \pvPi_\pm&:=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig).
\end{align*}
The root is the principal scalar root.  Thus $\bu$ is a
\hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one};
$\bu\cdot\bsig$ is \hyperref[eq:involutory-paravector]{self-inverse}.
Its square gives
\begin{align*}
 \pvPi_\pm^2&=\tfrac14(1\pm2\bu\cdot\bsig+1)=\pvPi_\pm,\\
 \pvPi_+\pvPi_-&=\tfrac14(1-(\bu\cdot\bsig)^2)=0,\\
 \pvPi_++\pvPi_-&=1,\\
 \pZ&=c(\pvPi_++\pvPi_-)+w(\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-)
   =\lambda_+\pvPi_++\lambda_-\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
Here $w,\lambda_\pm\in\C$, $\bu\in\C^3$, and
$\pZ,f(\pZ)\in\C\oplus\C^3$ for the analytic functions below.
These projectors are Hermitian when $\bu$ is real; in general they need
not be. The same formulas apply directly to any given
$\pZ=c+z\bu\cdot\bsig$, with $c,z\in\C$ and $\bu^2=1$.
If $z=0$, $\pZ=c$ is scalar and $f(\pZ)=f(c)$.
The complementary-projector
identities give, for every
nonnegative integer $n$,
\[
 \pZ^n=\lambda_+^n\pvPi_++\lambda_-^n\pvPi_-
 =\tfrac12(\lambda_+^n+\lambda_-^n)
 +\tfrac12(\lambda_+^n-\lambda_-^n)\bu\cdot\bsig.
\]
For a scalar power series $f(z)=\sum_{n=0}^\infty c_nz^n$ converging at
both eigenvalues, with $c_n\in\C$, inserting these powers into $f(\pZ)=\sum_n c_n\pZ^n$
gives the spectral rule~\citeat{higham-functions}{p.~3}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pZ)=f(\lambda_+)\pvPi_++f(\lambda_-)\pvPi_-.
 \label{eq:complex-function-projectors}
\end{equation}
The same rule defines chosen analytic branches at the two eigenvalues.
Its proof uses only complementary idempotents and therefore applies to
any decomposition with these projector identities.

Adjugation exchanges the eigenvalues, so
\begin{align*}
 \adj {\pZ}&=\lambda_-\pvPi_++\lambda_+\pvPi_-,\\
 \det(\pZ)&=\lambda_+\lambda_-,\\
 \pZ^{-1}&=\frac{\adj {\pZ}}{\det(\pZ)}
       =\lambda_+^{-1}\pvPi_++\lambda_-^{-1}\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
The inverse requires both eigenvalues to be nonzero.  Independently choosing
the signs of their principal square roots gives
\begin{equation*}
 \sqrt {\pZ}=\pm\sqrt{\lambda_+}\pvPi_+\pm\sqrt{\lambda_-}\pvPi_-.
\end{equation*}
There are four distinct roots when both eigenvalues are nonzero, and two
when one vanishes.  Changing $w$ to $-w$ exchanges the eigenvalue--projector
pairs and leaves the function unchanged when its branch choices are
exchanged with them.

\subsection{Square roots and repeated eigenvalues}

\subsubsection{Real paravectors and direct square roots}
For real $\pX=a+\bX\cdot\bsig$, with $a\in\R$, $\bX\in\R^3$, and
$\bX\ne0$, the spectral quantities above reduce to
\[
 w=\lVert\bX\rVert,\qquad
 \bu=\bX/\lVert\bX\rVert,\qquad
 \lambda_\pm=a\pm\lVert\bX\rVert.
\]
Here $\pX,\pvPi_\pm\in\R\oplus\R^3$, $w,\lambda_\pm\in\R$,
and $\bu\in\R^3$. The projectors are now Hermitian; complex-valued
functions or square roots may still give $f(\pX)\in\C\oplus\C^3$.  In particular, for a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$,
\begin{align*}
 f(\bu\cdot\bsig)
 &=f(1)\pvPi_++f(-1)\pvPi_-\\
 &=\tfrac12(f(1)+f(-1))
  +\tfrac12(f(1)-f(-1))\bu\cdot\bsig,\\
 \sqrt{\bu\cdot\bsig}&=\pm\pvPi_+\pm\ii\pvPi_-.
\end{align*}
The two root signs are independent.  For a fixed analytic branch,
$f(\pX)\to f(a)$ as $\bX\to0$; independent root choices require the
scalar case below.

A direct component derivation also determines all \emph{real} roots.
Let $\pY=b+\bY\cdot\bsig$ be real, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$,
and seek real $\pX=a+\bX\cdot\bsig$ such that $\pX^2=\pY$.  Equating parts gives
\begin{align*}
 a^2+\bX^2&=b,\\
 2a\bX&=\bY.
\end{align*}
If $\bY\ne0$, then $a\ne0$ and $\bX=\bY/(2a)$.  Substitution gives
\begin{align}
 a^4-ba^2+\tfrac14\bY^2&=0, \notag\\
 a^2&=\tfrac12\left(b\pm\sqrt{b^2-\bY^2}\right).
 \label{eq:real-square-root-scalar}
\end{align}
For each nonnegative $a^2$ in \eqref{eq:real-square-root-scalar}, take both
scalar roots $a$ and set
$\bX=\bY/(2a)$; then $\bX^2=b-a^2$.  There are four real roots when
$b>\lVert\bY\rVert$, two when $b=\lVert\bY\rVert>0$, and none when
$b<\lVert\bY\rVert$.

If $\bY=0$, the equations instead give the scalar roots
$\pX=\pm\sqrt b$ and the pure-vector roots $\pX=\bX\cdot\bsig$ with
$\bX^2=b$ for $b>0$.  For $b=0$ only $\pX=0$ is real; for $b<0$ there
is no real root.

\subsubsection{Coalescing eigenvalues}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ with $\bZ^2=0$, define
$\pN=\bZ\cdot\bsig\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$, so $\pN^2=0$.  A scalar function analytic at $c$
has the terminating Taylor expansion~\citeat{higham-functions}{p.~4}{page=5}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pZ)=f(c)+f'(c)\pN.
 \label{eq:coalescing-function}
\end{equation}
For $c\ne0$, the chosen square root and logarithm are
\begin{align*}
 \sqrt {\pZ}&=r+\frac{\pN}{2r},
 \\
 \log \pZ&=\log c+\frac{\pN}{c}.
\end{align*}
Here $r\in\C$ is a chosen scalar root of $c$ ($r^2=c$); use a consistent
logarithm branch.  A nonzero $\pN$ with $c=0$ has no square
root: such a root would have only zero eigenvalues, and its quadratic
identity would force its square to vanish.  It has no logarithm because
it is not invertible.  If $\pN=0$, a fixed analytic
branch gives $f(c)$; non-scalar square roots of a scalar must instead be
found from the component equations $a^2+\bX^2=c$, $2a\bX=0$, now allowing
$a\in\C$ and $\bX\in\C^3$.  Independent branch choices need not remain
finite as distinct eigenvalues coalesce.

\section{Exponentials and elementary functions}
\label{sec:elementary-functions}

The spectral rule gives explicit functions and the factors used for
rotations and boosts. Their composition and logarithms complete the link
between algebraic functions and geometric transformations.

\subsection{Exponential: projection and series derivations}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ with $\bZ^2\ne0$, recall
$w=+\sqrt{\bZ^2}$, the \hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one}
$\bu=\bZ/w$, and $\lambda_\pm=c\pm w$.
The spectral rule~\eqref{eq:complex-function-projectors} gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ)
 &=\tfrac12e^{c+w}(1+\bu\cdot\bsig)
  +\tfrac12e^{c-w}(1-\bu\cdot\bsig)\\
 &=e^c\left(\frac{e^w+e^{-w}}2
      +\frac{e^w-e^{-w}}2\bu\cdot\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Independently, $c$ commutes with the vector part and
$(w\bu\cdot\bsig)^{2n}=w^{2n}$,
$(w\bu\cdot\bsig)^{2n+1}=w^{2n+1}\bu\cdot\bsig$.  Separating the
defining series therefore gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ)&=e^c\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}
      \frac{(w\bu\cdot\bsig)^n}{n!}\\
 &=e^c\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{2n}}{(2n)!}
       +\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{w^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}\bu\cdot\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Both derivations give~\citeat{chappell-functions}{Eq.~(52)}{page=12}
\begin{equation}
 \exp(\pZ)=e^c(\cosh w+\sinh w\,\bu\cdot\bsig).
 \label{eq:paravector-exponential}
\end{equation}
This is independent of the choice $w$ or $-w$: $\cosh$ is even, while
both $\sinh w$ and $\bu$ change sign.  When $\bZ^2=0$,
\eqref{eq:coalescing-function} instead gives
\[
 \exp(c+\bZ\cdot\bsig)=e^c(1+\bZ\cdot\bsig).
\]
The defining series also gives
\begin{align*}
 \exp(\pZ^*)&=\exp(\pZ)^*,\\
 \exp(\pZ^{\mathsf H})&=\exp(\pZ)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{align*}

For the real components $a,b,\bX,\bY$ of
$\pZ=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig$, write
\[
 w=+\sqrt{\bX^2-\bY^2+2\ii\bX\cdot\bY}.
\]
Substituting $\bu=(\bX+\ii\bY)/w$ into
\eqref{eq:paravector-exponential} gives the component form
\begin{equation*}
 \exp(\pZ)=e^a(\cos b+\ii\sin b)
 \left(\cosh w+\frac{\sinh w}{w}(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig\right).
\end{equation*}
Its real and imaginary specializations are
\begin{align}
 \exp(a+\bX\cdot\bsig)
 &=e^a\left(\cosh\lVert\bX\rVert
  +\frac{\sinh\lVert\bX\rVert}{\lVert\bX\rVert}\bX\cdot\bsig\right),
 \label{eq:real-exponential}\\
 \exp\!\left(\ii(b+\bY\cdot\bsig)\right)
 &=(\cos b+\ii\sin b)\left(\cos\lVert\bY\rVert
  +\ii\frac{\sin\lVert\bY\rVert}{\lVert\bY\rVert}\bY\cdot\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:imaginary-exponential}
\end{align}
Setting $a=0$ in \eqref{eq:real-exponential} or $b=0$ in
\eqref{eq:imaginary-exponential} gives the pure-vector exponentials.
Every quotient uses its analytic limit at a zero argument.

\subsection{Rotation and boost factors}
\label{sec:algebra-rotation-boost}
For a general real exponent $a+\bX\cdot\bsig$, where $a\in\R$ and
$\bX\in\R^3$, \eqref{eq:real-exponential} gives
\begin{equation}
 \det\!\left(\exp(a+\bX\cdot\bsig)\right)=e^{2a}.
 \label{eq:exponential-determinant}
\end{equation}
Thus a real exponential has determinant one exactly when its scalar
exponent is zero.  Its value still has a nonzero scalar part, supplied
by the hyperbolic cosine.

Let $\theta\in\R$ and let $\bu\in\R^3$ be a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector},
$\bu^2=1$.  The two special exponentials just derived define the complex
rotation factor $\pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$ and the real boost factor
$\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(6)}{page=4}\,\citeat{baylis-intro}{p.~23}{page=23}:
\begin{equation}
 \pR(\theta,\bu):=\exp\!\left(-\ii\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig\right)
 =\cos\frac\theta2-\ii\sin\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:rotation-exponential}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \pL(\theta,\bu):=\exp\!\left(-\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig\right)
 =\cosh\frac\theta2-\sinh\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-exponential}
\end{equation}
In \eqref{eq:rotation-exponential} and \eqref{eq:boost-exponential},
the parameter is a rotation angle for $\pR$ and a rapidity for $\pL$; both are
real dimensionless numbers.  Here these names specify algebraic objects.
Their actions on a general paravector are derived in
Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}; their physical applications follow in
Sections~\ref{sec:inertial-frames} and~\ref{sec:transformations}.

Because $(\bu\cdot\bsig)^2=1$, reversing the parameter gives the
inverses
\begin{align}
 \pR^{-1}&=\pR(-\theta,\bu)
 =\cos\frac\theta2+\ii\sin\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig,
 \notag\\
 \pL^{-1}&=\pL(-\theta,\bu)
 =\cosh\frac\theta2+\sinh\frac\theta2\bu\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-inverse}
\end{align}
Sigma conjugation changes both $\ii$ and $\bsig$ in sign; Hermitian
conjugation changes $\ii$ but fixes a real vector.  Hence
\begin{align}
 \pR^*&=\pR, \notag\\
 \pR^{\mathsf H}&=\pR^{-1}, \label{eq:rotation-H}\\
 \pL^*&=\pL^{-1}, \notag\\
 \pL^{\mathsf H}&=\pL. \label{eq:boost-H}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:rotation-H} makes $\pR$
\hyperref[eq:unitary-transform]{unitary}; \eqref{eq:boost-H}
makes $\pL$ Hermitian, so $\pL^{\mathsf H}\pL=\pL^2$.  Their determinants and
squared norms follow from the circular and hyperbolic identities:
\begin{align}
 \det(\pR)&=\cos^2(\theta/2)+\sin^2(\theta/2)=1,
 \label{eq:rotation-determinant}\\
 \det(\pL)&=\cosh^2(\theta/2)-\sinh^2(\theta/2)=1,
 \label{eq:boost-determinant}\\
 \lVert \pR\rVert^2&=1, \label{eq:rotation-norm}\\
 \lVert \pL\rVert^2&=\cosh\theta. \label{eq:boost-norm}
\end{align}
Equations~\eqref{eq:rotation-determinant} and \eqref{eq:boost-determinant}
make both \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{paravectors of determinant one},
so their adjugates equal their inverses.  Equations~\eqref{eq:rotation-norm}
and \eqref{eq:boost-norm} distinguish their squared norms; only $\pR$ is
unitary for nonzero $\theta$.  The half-angle gives the sign and period:
\begin{align}
 \pR(\theta+2\pi,\bu)&=-\pR(\theta,\bu),
 \label{eq:rotation-two-pi}\\
 \pR(\theta+4\pi,\bu)&=+\pR(\theta,\bu).
 \notag
\end{align}

\paragraph{Gamma form.}
Recall $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential}.  Define its real velocity parameter and
Lorentz factor by
\begin{align*}
 \beta&:=\tanh\theta,
 \\
 \gamma&:=\cosh\theta=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\beta^2}}.
\end{align*}
Here $-1<\beta<1$, $\gamma\ge1$, and $\gamma\beta=\sinh\theta$.
The half-angle identities give
\begin{align*}
 \cosh\frac\theta2&=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}},\\
 \sinh\frac\theta2&=\frac{\gamma\beta}{\sqrt{2(\gamma+1)}}.
\end{align*}
Hence the boost factor itself is
\begin{equation}
 \pL=\sqrt{\frac{\gamma+1}{2}}
 \left(1-\frac{\gamma\beta}{\gamma+1}\bu\cdot\bsig\right).
 \label{eq:boost-gamma-form}
\end{equation}
Squaring doubles the exponent, so the full-rapidity paravector is
\begin{equation}
 \pL^2=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig)
 =\gamma(1-\beta\bu\cdot\bsig).
 \label{eq:boost-square}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-square} has prefactor $\gamma$; $\pL$ has the half-angle
prefactor in \eqref{eq:boost-gamma-form}.  Equivalently, for any real vector
$\bX\ne0$, put $\bv=\tanh\lVert\bX\rVert\,\bX/\lVert\bX\rVert$ and
$\gamma=\cosh\lVert\bX\rVert$.  Then
\[
 \exp(\bX\cdot\bsig)=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig).
\]
The zero-vector case follows by continuity.  This last real paravector has
\hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{determinant one} and represents four-velocity in
Section~\ref{sec:kinematics}.

\subsection{Cayley transform}
Let $\pY=b+\bY\cdot\bsig$ be real, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$, and
$0<\lVert\bY\rVert<b$.  Set the \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector}
$\bu=\bY/\lVert\bY\rVert$ and recall
$\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig)/2$.  Its sigma conjugate
$\pY^*=b-\bY\cdot\bsig=\adj {\pY}$ commutes with $\pY$, so
\[
 \frac{\pY}{\pY^*}=\frac{\pY^2}{\det(\pY)}
 =\frac{b^2+\bY^2+2b\bY\cdot\bsig}{b^2-\bY^2}.
\]
Define the real parameter $\theta=2\tanh^{-1}(\lVert\bY\rVert/b)$.
The double-angle identities give
\begin{align*}
 \cosh\theta&=\frac{b^2+\bY^2}{b^2-\bY^2},\\
 \sinh\theta&=\frac{2b\lVert\bY\rVert}{b^2-\bY^2}.
\end{align*}
Thus the quotient is the full-rapidity exponential
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{b+\bY\cdot\bsig}{b-\bY\cdot\bsig}
 &=\cosh\theta+\sinh\theta\,\bu\cdot\bsig\\
 &=\exp(\theta\bu\cdot\bsig)
 =e^\theta\pvPi_++e^{-\theta}\pvPi_-.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:cayley-exponential}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:cayley-exponential} is $\pL(\theta,\bu)^{-2}$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-square}.  Its real generator
vector is $\bX=\theta\bu$, while the Cayley vector obeys
\begin{equation*}
 \bY=b\tanh\!\left(\frac{\lVert\bX\rVert}{2}\right)
       \frac{\bX}{\lVert\bX\rVert}.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\lVert\bY\rVert/b=\tanh(\theta/2)$, the half-rapidity
parameter.  The corresponding real Lorentz factor and velocity vector are
\begin{align*}
 \gamma&=\frac{b^2+\bY^2}{b^2-\bY^2},\\
 \bv&=\frac{2b\bY}{b^2+\bY^2}.
\end{align*}
They give the equivalent forms
\[
 \exp(\bX\cdot\bsig)=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig)
 =\frac{b+\bY\cdot\bsig}{b-\bY\cdot\bsig}=\frac{\pY}{\pY^*}.
\]
Choosing $b=1$ normalizes the scalar part of $\pY$ to one; $\bY=0$ follows by
continuity.

\subsection{Composing the factors}
Recall $\pL(\theta,\bu)=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ and
$\pR(\theta,\bu)=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential} and \eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}.
For a fixed \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit direction} $\bu$
and real parameters $\theta,\theta'$, the
generators commute, so their parameters add:
\begin{equation}
 \pL(\theta,\bu)\pL(\theta',\bu)=\pL(\theta+\theta',\bu),
 \label{eq:boost-composition}
\end{equation}
\begin{align*}
 \pR(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)&=\pR(\theta+\theta',\bu),\\
 \pL(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)&=\pR(\theta',\bu)\pL(\theta,\bu).
\end{align*}
For the aligned real and imaginary generator, put
$w=-(\theta+\ii\theta')/2\in\C$.  Its exponential is
\[
 \exp(w\bu\cdot\bsig)
 =\pL(\theta,\bu)\pR(\theta',\bu)
 =\cosh w+\sinh w\,\bu\cdot\bsig.
\]
Expanding the complex scalar coefficients gives
\begin{align*}
 \cosh w&=\cosh\frac\theta2\cos\frac{\theta'}2
 +\ii\sinh\frac\theta2\sin\frac{\theta'}2,\\
 \sinh w&=-\sinh\frac\theta2\cos\frac{\theta'}2
 -\ii\cosh\frac\theta2\sin\frac{\theta'}2.
\end{align*}
This aligned factorization requires parallel real and imaginary generator
vectors.  For different \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit directions}
$\bu,\bv$, the vector product
instead gives
\begin{equation}
 \left[-\tfrac12\bu\cdot\bsig,-\tfrac12\bv\cdot\bsig\right]
 =\tfrac\ii2(\bu\times\bv)\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:boost-generator-commutator}
\end{equation}
This commutator is a rotation generator:
boosts along nonparallel directions do not commute.

\subsection{Logarithm}
For complex $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ with $\bZ^2\ne0$, recall
$w=+\sqrt{\bZ^2}$, the \hyperref[eq:bilinear-unit-vector]{vector of square one}
$\bu=\bZ/w$, $\lambda_\pm=c\pm w$, and
$\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig)/2$.  Assume both eigenvalues are nonzero.
For a nonzero complex scalar $\lambda$ and any integer $n$, the scalar branches are
\[
 \log\lambda=\log\lVert\lambda\rVert+\ii(\arg\lambda+2n\pi).
\]
Choose one branch for each eigenvalue.  The spectral rule gives
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \log \pZ&=\log(\lambda_+)\pvPi_++\log(\lambda_-)\pvPi_-\\
 &=\tfrac12\bigl(\log\lambda_++\log\lambda_-\bigr)
 +\tfrac12\bigl(\log\lambda_+-\log\lambda_-\bigr)\bu\cdot\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:paravector-logarithm}
\end{equation}
Exchanging $w$ and $-w$ exchanges both eigenvalue--projector pairs and
preserves the same chosen logarithm.  Independent scalar branches give
the different logarithms.

For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$,
\eqref{eq:paravector-logarithm} uses
eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$, giving, for integers $n,m$,
\begin{equation*}
 \log(\bu\cdot\bsig)
 =2n\pi\ii\pvPi_++(2m+1)\pi\ii\pvPi_-.
\end{equation*}
For real $\pY=b+\bY\cdot\bsig$, with $b\in\R$, $\bY\in\R^3$, and
$b>\lVert\bY\rVert$, both eigenvalues are positive.  Combining their real
logarithms yields
\begin{equation*}
 \log \pY=\tfrac12\log(b^2-\bY^2)
 +\tanh^{-1}\!\left(\frac{\lVert\bY\rVert}{b}\right)
       \frac{\bY}{\lVert\bY\rVert}\cdot\bsig.
\end{equation*}
The vector term vanishes at $\bY=0$; the general coalescing case
was treated above.

\section{Transformations and invariants}
\label{sec:preserved-quantities}

Here $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ is a complex paravector: $c\in\C$ and
$\bZ\in\C^3$, hence $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
Transformation factors $\pT,\pT'\in\C\oplus\C^3$ are constant.
Distinguish the scalar part $\scpart {\pZ}=c=\tfrac12\operatorname{tr}\pZ$
from \eqref{eq:trace-scalar}, the complex determinant $c^2-\bZ^2$ from
\eqref{eq:det}, and the squared norm $\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ}$ from
\eqref{eq:hermitian-norm}.  Scalar part and trace carry exactly the same
information; we use $\operatorname{sc}$ below.

\subsection{Similarity, determinant, and norm}

For a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$,
similarity means $\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}$.
Scalar-part cyclicity gives
\begin{equation*}
 \scpart{\pZ'}=\scpart{\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}}
 =\scpart{\pZ \pT^{-1}\pT}=\scpart {\pZ}.
\end{equation*}
Thus trace is also preserved.  Multiplicativity gives
\begin{equation}
 \det(\pZ')=\det(\pT)\det(\pZ)\det(\pT^{-1})=\det(\pZ).
 \label{eq:det-similarity}
\end{equation}
The opposite similarity $\pZ'=\pT^{-1}\pZ \pT$ has the same invariants.
For independent complex factors $\pT,\pT'$, the law $\pZ'=\pT'\pZ \pT$ instead gives
\begin{equation*}
 \det(\pZ')=\det(\pT')\det(\pT)\det(\pZ).
\end{equation*}
This preserves the determinant for
every $\pZ$ exactly when $\det(\pT')\det(\pT)=1$.  This alone does not preserve
scalar part, norm, or the real paravector subspace.

If the factor $\pT$ is unitary, then $\pT^{\mathsf H}=\pT^{-1}$ by
\eqref{eq:unitary-transform}.  For $\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}$, cancellation and
scalar-part cyclicity give
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \lVert \pZ'\rVert^2
 &=\scpart{\pT \pZ^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\scpart{\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}\pT}=\lVert \pZ\rVert^2.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The norm is also preserved by $\pZ'=\pT \pZ$ and $\pZ'=\pZ \pT$.
The congruence additionally preserves scalar part and determinant;
a one-sided unitary product need not.

\subsubsection{Determinant one is not Hermitian unitarity}
\label{sec:determinant-versus-unitarity}

Invertibility, determinant one, and Hermitian unitarity are distinct
conditions. Invertibility means that a two-sided inverse exists, as in
\eqref{eq:unit-paravector}; determinant one means $\det\pZ=1$; and
Hermitian unitarity means $\pZ^{\mathsf H}\pZ=1$, as in
\eqref{eq:unitary-transform}. The phrase \emph{unit determinant} means
only that the determinant equals one. It does not mean unit norm or
Hermitian unitarity.

Determinant one implies invertibility, but does not imply Hermitian unitarity.
To distinguish them, take a real paravector $\pX=a+\bX\cdot\bsig$
with $a\in\R$, $\bX\in\R^3$, and $a^2-\bX^2=1$.  Then
\[
 \pX^*\pX=\det\pX=1,\qquad
 \pX^{-1}=\pX^*=a-\bX\cdot\bsig.
\]
Thus determinant one implies invertibility.  Hermitian conjugation fixes
$\pX$, whereas sigma conjugation reverses its vector part, so
\begin{align*}
 \pX^{\mathsf H}\pX&=\pX^2=a^2+\bX^2+2a\bX\cdot\bsig,\\
 \lVert\pX\rVert^2&=a^2+\bX^2=1+2\bX^2.
\end{align*}
Both equal $1$ only when $\bX=0$, in which case $\pX=\pm1$.
Choosing any nonzero real $\bX$ and $a=\sqrt{1+\bX^2}$ therefore gives
an invertible paravector of determinant one that is not unitary.
These properties follow from the defining equations, not from the
letter used for the paravector.

\subsection{Real paravectors and the Lorentz metric}

Let $\pX=a+\bX\cdot\bsig$ be real, with $a\in\R$ and
$\bX\in\R^3$.  The determinant and squared norm give different quadratic forms:
\begin{align*}
 \det(\pX)&=a^2-\bX^2,\\
 \lVert \pX\rVert^2&=a^2+\bX^2.
\end{align*}
The first is the Lorentz quadratic form of signature $(+,-,-,-)$;
the second is the positive sum of squared coefficients.  A real-linear
isometry of the first form must preserve the real subspace and its
determinant.  For a constant complex
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$ with $\det(\pT)=1$,
use the Hermitian congruence~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(17)}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pX'=\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}.}
 \label{eq:general-lorentz-congruence}
\end{equation}
Hermitian conjugation and determinant multiplication give
\begin{align*}
 \pX'^{\mathsf H}&=\pT \pX^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}=\pX',\\
 \det(\pX')&=\det(\pT)\det(\pX)\det(\pT^{\mathsf H})=\det(\pX).
\end{align*}
For a second real paravector $\pY=b+\bY\cdot\bsig$, with $b\in\R$
and $\bY\in\R^3$, the associated metric pairing is~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(15)}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 \scpart{\pX^*\pY}=ab-\bX\cdot\bY
 =\tfrac12\bigl(\det(\pX+\pY)-\det(\pX)-\det(\pY)\bigr).
 \label{eq:lorentz-pairing}
\end{equation}
Linearity and determinant invariance therefore give
$\scpart{\pX'^*\pY'}=\scpart{\pX^*\pY}$: the full metric is preserved.
These maps preserve the positive cone as well.  For
$a\geq\lVert\bX\rVert$, the eigenvalues
$a\pm\lVert\bX\rVert$ are nonnegative, so $\pX$ has a real square root and
\[
 \scpart{\pX'}=\lVert\sqrt {\pX}\,\pT^{\mathsf H}\rVert^2\geq0.
\]
Together with determinant invariance, this keeps $\pX'$ in the cone
$a\geq\lVert\bX\rVert$.  These are the proper Lorentz transformations
preserving that cone; $\pT$ and $-\pT$ give the same map.

\paragraph{Why the scalar exponent vanishes.}
More generally, the congruence multiplies $\det(\pX)$ by
$\lVert\det(\pT)\rVert^2$.  For real $\pT=\exp(a+\bX\cdot\bsig)$,
\eqref{eq:exponential-determinant} therefore gives
\[
 \det(\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H})=e^{4a}\det(\pX).
\]
A nonzero real scalar exponent dilates the metric; it is excluded by
determinant invariance.  An imaginary scalar exponent contributes a phase
$e^{\ii b}$, $b\in\R$, which cancels between $\pT$ and $\pT^{\mathsf H}$.
Requiring $\det(\pT)=1$ removes this continuous phase freedom.
Thus a pure boost needs only a real vector exponent.

\paragraph{Why the squared norm is different.}
For $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, with real $\theta$ and real unit
$\bu$, applying \eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} to $\pX=1$ gives
\begin{align*}
 \pX'&=\pL^2=\cosh\theta-\sinh\theta\bu\cdot\bsig,\\
 \det(\pX')&=1,\\
 \scpart{\pX'}&=\cosh\theta,\\
 \lVert \pX'\rVert^2&=\cosh(2\theta).
\end{align*}
A nonzero boost preserves the determinant while changing the scalar part
and squared norm.  In fact, a determinant-one congruence preserves the
squared norm for every real $\pX$ exactly when $\pT$ is
\hyperref[eq:unitary-transform]{unitary}.  Necessity follows by taking
$\pX=1$: the real, positive $\pT \pT^{\mathsf H}$ can have both determinant and
norm one only if it is $1$, which makes $\pT$ unitary.  Sufficiency was proved above.
Normalizing $\pL$ to $\lVert \pL\rVert=1$ would change its determinant
and lose the Lorentz metric.

\paragraph{Every factor separates into a boost and a rotation.}
For a determinant-one complex $\pT$, the paravector $\pT \pT^{\mathsf H}$
is real, has determinant one, and has positive scalar part
$\lVert \pT\rVert^2$.  Both eigenvalues are therefore positive.
Using the positive real square root, define
\[
 \pB:=\sqrt{\pT \pT^{\mathsf H}},\qquad \pR:=\pB^{-1}\pT.
\]
Then $\det(\pB)=1$,
$\pR^{\mathsf H}\pR=1$, $\det(\pR)=1$, and $\pT=\pB \pR$, with
$\pB\in\R\oplus\R^3$ and $\pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$.
The real logarithm of $\pB$ has zero scalar part by
\eqref{eq:exponential-determinant}, so $\pB$ is a real vector exponential.
For $\pR$, the identity $\pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1}=\adj {\pR}$ makes its scalar
coefficient real and its vector coefficient imaginary; determinant one
then gives $\pR=\cos(\theta/2)-\ii\sin(\theta/2)\bu\cdot\bsig$
for some real $\theta$ and real unit $\bu$.
Consequently $\pT \pX \pT^{\mathsf H}=\pB \pR \pX \pR^{\mathsf H}\pB$: the rotation is
followed by the boost.  Their component actions follow next.

\subsection{Rotation action and Rodrigues' formula}
\label{sec:algebra-rotation-action}

Recall the unitary factor
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}, with real angle $\theta$ and
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis} $\bu$.
For the complex paravector $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$, use the Hermitian congruence~\citeat{baylis2004}{Eq.~(5)}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pZ'=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}.}
 \label{eq:rotation-action}
\end{equation}
Since $\pR^{\mathsf H}=\pR^{-1}$ by \eqref{eq:rotation-H}, this keeps $c'=c$.
The vector product
\eqref{eq:vector-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 (\bu\cdot\bsig)(\bZ\cdot\bsig)(\bu\cdot\bsig)
 &=2(\bu\cdot\bZ)\bu\cdot\bsig-\bZ\cdot\bsig,\\
 [\bZ\cdot\bsig,\bu\cdot\bsig]
 &=-2\ii(\bu\times\bZ)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Inserting the sine and cosine forms of $\pR$ and $\pR^{\mathsf H}$ therefore yields
\begin{align*}
 \bZ'\cdot\bsig
 &={}\cos^2\frac\theta2\,\bZ\cdot\bsig
 +2\sin\frac\theta2\cos\frac\theta2
       (\bu\times\bZ)\cdot\bsig\\
 &\quad+\sin^2\frac\theta2
 \left(2(\bu\cdot\bZ)\bu\cdot\bsig-\bZ\cdot\bsig\right).
\end{align*}
Collecting terms gives Rodrigues' formula~\citeat{baylis-intro}{p.~14}{page=14}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bZ'=\bZ\cos\theta+(\bu\times\bZ)\sin\theta
 +(\bu\cdot\bZ)(1-\cos\theta)\bu.}
 \label{eq:rodrigues}
\end{equation}
This is the active right-handed rotation through $+\theta$ about $\bu$.
Rotating the coordinate axes through $+\theta$ instead uses the inverse map.
It preserves $\operatorname{sc}$, determinant, norm, and the real
paravector subspace. For complex $\bZ$, it rotates its real and imaginary
parts by the same real rotation.  The sign change in
\eqref{eq:rotation-two-pi} affects both factors in
\eqref{eq:rotation-action}, so $\pZ'$ has period $2\pi$.

\subsection{Quaternions as paravectors}
\label{sec:quaternions}

The quaternion units are already present in the complex vector part of
our algebra.  Define
\[
 \pvnu_k:=-\ii\pvsigma_k\quad(k=1,2,3),\qquad
 \bquatbasis:=-\ii\bsig=\{\pvnu_1,\pvnu_2,\pvnu_3\}.
\]
The sigma product gives $\pvnu_1^2=\pvnu_2^2=\pvnu_3^2=-1$,
$\pvnu_1\pvnu_2=\pvnu_3$, $\pvnu_2\pvnu_3=\pvnu_1$, and $\pvnu_3\pvnu_1=\pvnu_2$; reversing any
of these distinct pairs changes its sign.  Thus $\pvnu_1\pvnu_2\pvnu_3=-1$.
These are the three quaternion units, distinct from the commuting
complex scalar $\ii$.

For $a\in\R$ and $\bQ\in\R^3$, the quaternion representation is~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~92}{page=103}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pQ:=a+\bQ\cdot\bquatbasis=a-\ii\bQ\cdot\bsig.}
 \label{eq:quaternion-paravector}
\end{equation}
Here $\bQ\cdot\bquatbasis=Q_1\pvnu_1+Q_2\pvnu_2+Q_3\pvnu_3$.
In the decomposition $\pZ=(a'+b'\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig$, with
scalar part $c'=a'+b'\ii$, this selects $a'=a$, $b'=0$, $\bX=0$,
and $\bY=-\bQ$: equivalently, $c'=a$ and $\bZ=-\ii\bQ$.
Thus $\pQ,\pQ'\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3$: a real scalar coefficient and purely
imaginary vector coefficients.  Their four real components
form a subalgebra of the eight-real-component complex paravectors.
Indeed, for $\pQ'=a'+\bQ'\cdot\bquatbasis$, the paravector product becomes~\citeat{lounesto-intro}{p.~9}{page=22}
\begin{equation}
 \pQ \pQ'=(aa'-\bQ\cdot\bQ')
 +(a\bQ'+a'\bQ+\bQ\times\bQ')\cdot\bquatbasis.
 \label{eq:quaternion-product}
\end{equation}

Since $\pvnu_k^{\mathsf H}=-\pvnu_k$ and $\pvnu_k^*=\pvnu_k$, quaternion conjugation
is the existing Hermitian conjugation:
\begin{align}
 \pQ^{\mathsf H}&=a-\bQ\cdot\bquatbasis
 =a+\ii\bQ\cdot\bsig,\notag\\
 \pQ^*&=\pQ,\qquad \adj {\pQ}=\pQ^{\mathsf H},\notag\\
 \pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}&=\pQ^{\mathsf H}\pQ=a^2+\bQ^2
 =\det \pQ=\lVert \pQ\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:quaternion-norm}
\end{align}
For nonzero $\pQ$, \eqref{eq:quaternion-norm} gives
$\pQ^{-1}=\pQ^{\mathsf H}/(a^2+\bQ^2)$.
Nonzero quaternions are normalized with
\[
 \pQ\leftarrow\frac{\pQ}{\sqrt{\pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}}},
\]
which, with $\lVert\bQ\rVert^2=\bQ^2$ for real $\bQ$, gives
\[
 \pQ \pQ^{\mathsf H}=a^2+\lVert\bQ\rVert^2=1.
\]
Thus a unit quaternion has $\pQ^{-1}=\pQ^{\mathsf H}$.

To rotate $\br\in\R^3$, represent it by the pure quaternion
$\br\cdot\bquatbasis=-\ii\br\cdot\bsig$.
For a unit quaternion $\pQ=a+\bQ\cdot\bquatbasis$, use the quaternion sandwich:
\begin{equation}
 \br'\cdot\bquatbasis=\pQ(\br\cdot\bquatbasis)\pQ^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad
 \br'\cdot\bsig=\pQ(\br\cdot\bsig)\pQ^{\mathsf H}.
 \label{eq:quaternion-rotation}
\end{equation}
The two forms agree because $-\ii$ commutes with every paravector.
Applying \eqref{eq:quaternion-product} twice gives
\begin{align*}
 \pQ(\br\cdot\bquatbasis)
 &=-\bQ\cdot\br+(a\br+\bQ\times\br)\cdot\bquatbasis,\\
 \pQ(\br\cdot\bquatbasis)\pQ^{\mathsf H}
 &=\bigl((a^2-\bQ^2)\br+2(\bQ\cdot\br)\bQ
          +2a\bQ\times\br\bigr)\cdot\bquatbasis.
\end{align*}
The scalar part cancels. For a unit quaternion with $\bQ\ne0$, its
real unit axis $\bu$ and rotation angle $\theta$ satisfy
\[
 \bu=\frac{\bQ}{\lVert\bQ\rVert},\qquad
 a=\cos\frac\theta2,\qquad \bQ=\bu\sin\frac\theta2.
\]
Thus the quaternion is the rotation factor
\[
 \pQ=\cos\frac\theta2-\ii\bu\cdot\bsig\sin\frac\theta2=\pR.
\]
Then $a^2-\bQ^2=\cos\theta$,
$2a\bQ=\bu\sin\theta$, and
$2(\bQ\cdot\br)\bQ=(1-\cos\theta)(\bu\cdot\br)\bu$.
Consequently \eqref{eq:quaternion-rotation} recovers
\[
 \boxed{\br'=\br\cos\theta+(\bu\times\br)\sin\theta
       +(\bu\cdot\br)(1-\cos\theta)\bu.}
\]
This is precisely Rodrigues' formula~\eqref{eq:rodrigues} for
$\bZ=\br$.  Thus $\pR=\pQ$, and the quaternion sandwich is exactly
$\pR(\br\cdot\bsig)\pR^{\mathsf H}$ from \eqref{eq:rotation-action}.
The reversed sandwich $\pQ^{\mathsf H}(\br\cdot\bsig)\pQ$ gives the inverse rotation.

\subsection{Boost action and its sign convention}
\label{sec:algebra-boost-action}

Recall $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-exponential}; $\theta\in\R$, $\bu\in\R^3$, $\bu^2=1$,
$\beta=\tanh\theta$, and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
Use the same congruence with $\pT=\pL$ and $\pL^{\mathsf H}=\pL$ from \eqref{eq:boost-H}~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(24)}{page=20}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pZ'=\pL \pZ \pL.}
 \label{eq:boost-action}
\end{equation}
The paravector $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig\in\C\oplus\C^3$ may be complex;
the congruence also preserves its real subspace.
Resolve $\bZ\in\C^3$ into parallel and perpendicular parts in $\C^3$:
\begin{align*}
 \bZ_{\parallel}&:=(\bu\cdot\bZ)\bu,\\
 \bZ_{\perp}&:=\bZ-\bZ_{\parallel}.
\end{align*}
The parallel term commutes with $\bu\cdot\bsig$; the perpendicular term
anticommutes with it.  Consequently,
\begin{align*}
 \pL(c+\bZ_{\parallel}\cdot\bsig)\pL
 &=(c+\bZ_{\parallel}\cdot\bsig)\pL^2,\\
 \pL(\bZ_{\perp}\cdot\bsig)\pL
 &=\bZ_{\perp}\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
\Needspace{7\baselineskip}
Using $\pL^2=\gamma(1-\beta\bu\cdot\bsig)$ from
\eqref{eq:boost-square}, multiplication gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.1)}{page=104}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{c'=\gamma(c-\beta\bu\cdot\bZ),}
 \label{eq:boost-scalar-component}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bZ'=\bZ_{\perp}+\gamma(\bZ_{\parallel}-\beta c\bu).}
 \label{eq:boost-vector-component}
\end{equation}
The equivalent longitudinal combinations expose the invariant directly:
\begin{equation}
 c'\pm\bu\cdot\bZ'=e^{\mp\theta}(c\pm\bu\cdot\bZ).
 \label{eq:boost-null-components}
\end{equation}
Their product is $c^2-(\bu\cdot\bZ)^2$; the transverse part is unchanged.
Thus \eqref{eq:boost-null-components} preserves $c^2-\bZ^2$ and, for real
$\pZ$, the null cone $c^2-\bZ^2=0$.

The inverse map is $\pZ=\pL^{-1}\pZ'\pL^{-1}$, obtained by reversing $\theta$.
Generally $\pL \pX\ne \pX \pL$: the scalar and parallel parts commute with $\pL$,
while a nonzero perpendicular part does not for a nonzero boost.
For example, with $\bu\cdot\bsig=\pvsigma_3$ and $\pX=\pvsigma_1$,
\[
 \pL \pX=\cosh(\theta/2)\pvsigma_1-\ii\sinh(\theta/2)\pvsigma_2,
 \qquad \pL \pX \pL=\pvsigma_1.
\]
The second factor cancels the imaginary transverse term.  Defining a
full-rapidity factor cannot make a single left product boost every real $\pX$;
it would merely replace the two factors by their square roots.

\paragraph{Similarity action of the boost factor.}
For $\pZ'=\pL \pZ \pL^{-1}$, the same commutation rules give $c'=c$ and
\begin{equation}
 \bZ'=\bZ_{\parallel}
 +\gamma\bigl(\bZ_{\perp}-\ii\beta\bu\times\bZ\bigr).
 \label{eq:boost-similarity-vector}
\end{equation}
This preserves scalar part and determinant, but fixes $\pZ=1$ and generally
makes a real vector complex.  It does not mix scalar and vector components
as the congruence does in
\eqref{eq:boost-scalar-component}--\eqref{eq:boost-vector-component}.
In particular, similarity preserves the scalar-free subspace;
a nonzero boost congruence gives a nonzero scalar part to
$\pZ=z\bu\cdot\bsig$ for any nonzero $z\in\C$.

\section{Space--time kinematics}
\label{sec:kinematics}

Let $\pX=t+\br\cdot\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3$ be an event, with $t\in\R$ and
$\br\in\R^3$.  A massive particle follows a future-directed
time-like path $\br(t)$; $s\in\R$ denotes its proper time.
Throughout this section,
\[
 \pX,\pU,\pP\in\R\oplus\R^3,\qquad
 \bv,\bp,\ba\in\R^3,\qquad E,m,\gamma\in\R,
\]
with $m>0$, $\bv^2<1$, $E>0$, and $\gamma\ge1$ for a massive particle.

\subsection{Intervals and proper time}

The differential event and its invariant interval are~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(7)}{page=8}
\begin{align}
 \dd \pX&=\dd t+\dd\br\cdot\bsig,\notag\\
 \dd s^2&=\det(\dd \pX)=\dd \pX\,\dd \pX^*
 =\dd t^2-\dd\br^2.
 \label{eq:interval}
\end{align}
By \eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence}, a constant complex
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$ with
\hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{$\det \pT=1$} gives
\[
 \dd \pX'=\pT\dd \pX \pT^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad \det(\dd \pX')=\dd s^2.
\]
The positive cone of Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities} now represents
future causal increments $\dd t\geq\lVert\dd\br\rVert$; the congruence
preserves both their interval and time orientation.
For a rotation, the event $\pX=t+\br\cdot\bsig$ transforms as
$\pX'=\pR\pX\pR^{\mathsf H}$.  Its scalar part gives $t'=t$, and
Rodrigues' formula~\eqref{eq:rodrigues} acts on the spatial position $\br$.

Positive, zero, and negative values of \eqref{eq:interval} mean time-like, null, and space-like
separation.  Massive particles follow time-like paths; light follows null
paths; causal signals cannot connect space-like separated events.
For two real intervals,
\begin{align*}
 [\dd \pX,\dd \pX']&=2\ii(\dd\br\times\dd\br')\cdot\bsig,\\
 (\dd \pX)^{-1}&=\frac{\dd \pX^*}{\dd s^2}\qquad(\dd s^2\ne0).
\end{align*}
A null interval has no inverse.

Along the massive path, define the real coordinate velocity
$\bv=\dd\br/\dd t$ and choose $\dd s>0$, with $\dd t>0$.
The interval gives
\begin{equation*}
 \dd s:=\sqrt{\dd t^2-\dd\br^2}=\sqrt{\det(\dd \pX)}.
\end{equation*}
Dividing by the positive coordinate-time increment gives
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\dd s}{\dd t}
 &=\sqrt{\frac{\dd t^2-\dd\br^2}{\dd t^2}}\\
 &=\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{\dd\br}{\dd t}\right)^2}
 =\sqrt{1-\bv^2}.
\end{align*}
Thus $\dd s^2=(1-\bv^2)\dd t^2$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.18)}{page=139}
\begin{align}
 \gamma&:=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}}=\frac{\dd t}{\dd s},
 \label{eq:lorentz-factor}\\
 \frac{\dd}{\dd s}&=\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t}.
 \label{eq:proper-time-derivative}
\end{align}
\subsection{Four-velocity and the mass shell}

Factor the coordinate-time increment out of the tangent and use
\eqref{eq:lorentz-factor} to obtain the real four-velocity~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(8)}{page=8}:
\begin{align}
 \pU:=\frac{\dd \pX}{\dd s}
 &=\frac{\dd t+\dd\br\cdot\bsig}{\dd s}\notag\\
 &=\left(1+\frac{\dd\br}{\dd t}\cdot\bsig\right)
   \frac{\dd t}{\dd s}\notag\\
 &=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig).
 \label{eq:proper-velocity}
\end{align}
Proper-time normalization gives
\[
 \det \pU=\det\!\left(\frac{\dd \pX}{\dd s}\right)=\frac{\det(\dd \pX)}{\dd s^2}=1.
\]
More explicitly, the interval gives
\begin{equation}
 \pU^*\pU=\frac{\dd\pX^*\dd\pX}{\dd s^2}=1.
 \label{eq:proper-velocity-star-normalization}
\end{equation}
Thus $\pU$ is an \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with
$\pU\pU^{-1}=\pU^{-1}\pU=1$.  Its two conjugates are
\begin{align*}
 \pU^{\mathsf H}&=\pU=\frac{\dd t+\dd\br\cdot\bsig}{\dd s},\\
 \pU^{-1}=\pU^*&=\frac{\dd t-\dd\br\cdot\bsig}{\dd s}
 =\gamma(1-\bv\cdot\bsig).
\end{align*}
This is the distinction between determinant one and Hermitian unitarity
from Section~\ref{sec:determinant-versus-unitarity}, applied to four-velocity:
\begin{align*}
 \pU^{\mathsf H}\pU&=\pU^2
 =\frac{\dd t^2+\dd\br^2+2\dd t\,\dd\br\cdot\bsig}{\dd s^2},\\
 \lVert\pU\rVert^2&=\frac{\dd t^2+\dd\br^2}{\dd t^2-\dd\br^2}.
\end{align*}
Both equal $1$ only when $\dd\br=0$, the particle's rest frame.
Proper-time normalization therefore gives determinant one in every
inertial frame, but Hermitian unitarity only at rest.
For a \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit direction} $\bu$, write $\bv=\beta\bu$ and
$\theta=\tanh^{-1}\beta$.  Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-square} gives $\pU=\pL^{-2}$ for
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$: the direct boost $\pL \pU \pL=1$
takes this tangent to rest.

For constant real mass $m>0$, define the real four-momentum~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.24)}{page=142}
\begin{equation}
 \pP:=m\pU=E+\bp\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:four-momentum}
\end{equation}
The energy, momentum, and inverse are
\begin{align*}
 E&=\scpart {\pP}=m\gamma,\\
 \bp&=\vecpart {\pP}=m\gamma\bv,\\
 \pP^{-1}&=\frac{E-\bp\cdot\bsig}{m^2}.
\end{align*}
Since $\det \pU=1$ and $m$ is a real scalar,
\[
 \det \pP=\det(m\pU)=m^2\det \pU=m^2.
\]
Thus the determinant gives the mass shell~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.30)}{page=143}
\begin{equation}
 E^2-\bp^2=\det \pP=m^2.
 \label{eq:mass-shell}
\end{equation}
For a massive particle of finite energy,
\[
 \bv^2=\frac{\bp^2}{E^2}=1-\frac{m^2}{E^2}<1.
\]
Approaching light speed requires unbounded energy.
A massless path has no proper-time velocity.  Define its momentum directly
as a future null paravector $\pP=E+\bp\cdot\bsig$, with $E>0$ and
$E^2=\bp^2$; it has no inverse.

\subsection{Four-acceleration and proper acceleration}
\label{sec:proper-acceleration}

The coordinate acceleration differentiates velocity with respect to coordinate
time $t$, whereas the four-acceleration differentiates four-velocity with
respect to proper time $s$.  Recall
\[
 \pU=\frac{\dd\pX}{\dd s}=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig),
 \qquad \gamma=(1-\bv^2)^{-1/2},
 \qquad \frac{\dd}{\dd s}=\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t},
\]
where $\bv=\dd\br/\dd t$ and $\ba=\dd\bv/\dd t\in\R^3$.
To differentiate $\pU$, first use the chain rule:
\[
 \frac{\dd\gamma}{\dd t}
 =-\tfrac12(1-\bv^2)^{-3/2}(-2\bv\cdot\ba)
 =\gamma^3(\bv\cdot\ba).
\]
The product rule then gives
\[
 \frac{\dd\pU}{\dd s}
 =\gamma\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\bigl[\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig)\bigr]
 =\gamma\frac{\dd\gamma}{\dd t}(1+\bv\cdot\bsig)
  +\gamma^2\ba\cdot\bsig.
\]
Substituting the derivative of $\gamma$ separates the scalar and vector
parts of the real four-acceleration paravector~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~141}{page=147}:
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}
 =\gamma^4(\bv\cdot\ba)
 +\left(\gamma^2\ba+\gamma^4(\bv\cdot\ba)\bv\right)\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:proper-acceleration-components}
\end{equation}
Four-velocity satisfies $\pU^*\pU=\det\pU=1$.
Differentiating this normalization with respect to proper time gives
\[
 \left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)^*\pU
 +\pU^*\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}=0.
\]
The two terms have equal real scalar parts, so
\begin{equation*}
 \scpart{\pU^*\dd \pU/\dd s}=0.
\end{equation*}
Thus four-velocity and four-acceleration are Minkowski-orthogonal.
Although $\dd\pU/\dd s$ can have a scalar part, the product
$\pU^*\dd\pU/\dd s$ is scalar-free and belongs to $\{0\}\oplus\C^3$.
Multiplication on the right by $\pU^*$ also gives
\begin{align*}
 \adj{\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}}
 &=-\pU^*\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\pU^*.
\end{align*}
Expanding the commutator and the product gives
\begin{align*}
 [\pU,\dd \pU/\dd s]
 &=2\ii\gamma^2\left(\bv\times\frac{\dd\bv}{\dd s}\right)\cdot\bsig,\\
 \pU^*\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}
 &=\gamma^3(\ba-\ii\bv\times\ba)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
The real vector part is proportional to $\ba$; the imaginary vector part
comes from the component of acceleration perpendicular to $\bv$.
Since $\det\pU^*=1$, multiplying by $\pU^*$ preserves the determinant.
The determinant of a scalar-free paravector is the negative of its square.
Using $\ba\cdot(\bv\times\ba)=0$ therefore gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)
 &=-\left(\pU^*\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)^2\\
 &=-\gamma^6\left(\ba^2-(\bv\times\ba)^2\right)\\
 &=-\gamma^4\left(\ba^2+\gamma^2(\bv\cdot\ba)^2\right).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The last equality uses
$(\bv\times\ba)^2=\bv^2\ba^2-(\bv\cdot\ba)^2$ and
$1-\bv^2=\gamma^{-2}$.  Nonzero four-acceleration is therefore space-like. The proper acceleration
is the acceleration measured in the instantaneous rest frame; its squared
magnitude is the negative determinant above.
For $\bv\ne0$, define
$\ba_{\parallel}=(\bv\cdot\ba)\bv/\bv^2$ and
$\ba_{\perp}=\ba-\ba_{\parallel}$.  Equivalently,
\begin{equation*}
 \det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =-\gamma^4\ba_{\perp}^2-\gamma^6\ba_{\parallel}^2.
\end{equation*}
At rest, $\gamma=1$ and $\dd\pU/\dd s=\ba\cdot\bsig$, so the
 determinant is $-\ba^2$ and the proper acceleration has magnitude $\lVert\ba\rVert$.

\subsubsection{Energy and inertia}
Recall $E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\bv$ for invariant real mass $m>0$.
Differentiating, using the derivative of $\gamma$ above, gives
\[
 \frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}
 =m\gamma\ba+m\gamma^3(\bv\cdot\ba)\bv.
\]
For $\bv\ne0$, resolving along and perpendicular to $\bv$ yields
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}
 =m\gamma\ba_{\perp}+m\gamma^3\ba_{\parallel}.}
 \label{eq:relativistic-inertia}
\end{equation}
Taking its dot product with $\bv$ gives the work rate
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd E}{\dd t}=m\gamma^3\bv\cdot\ba
 =\bv\cdot\frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}.
 \label{eq:relativistic-work}
\end{equation}
At rest the force is $m\ba$.  Restoring $c$, $E=\gamma mc^2$;
$E/c^2=\gamma m$ is sometimes called \emph{relativistic mass}.
We retain invariant $m$: the longitudinal and transverse inertia in
\eqref{eq:relativistic-inertia} differ.

\subsection{World lines, clocks, and light cones}
\label{sec:worldlines}

\subsubsection{Integrating four-velocity}
Recall the real path $\pX(s)=t(s)+\br(s)\cdot\bsig$ and its tangent
$\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector}
with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.  For real proper times $s_0<s_1$, integration gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pX(s_1)-\pX(s_0)=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\pU(s)\dd s.}
 \label{eq:worldline-integral}
\end{equation}
Its real components are
\begin{align*}
 t(s_1)-t(s_0)&=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\gamma(s)\dd s,\\
 \br(s_1)-\br(s_0)&=\int_{s_0}^{s_1}\gamma(s)\bv(s)\dd s.
\end{align*}
Thus between coordinate times $t_0$ and $t$ the clock records~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.19)}{page=140}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{s(t)-s(t_0)=\int_{t_0}^{t}\frac{\dd t'}{\gamma(t')}
 =\int_{t_0}^{t}\sqrt{1-\bv(t')^2}\dd t'.}
 \label{eq:elapsed-proper-time}
\end{equation}
This is the standard world-line proper-time integral.
For constant $\pU$, \eqref{eq:worldline-integral} is the straight line
$\pX(s)=\pX(s_0)+(s-s_0)\pU$.  For varying $\pU$, integrate the tangent;
an endpoint boost alone does not specify the path.

\subsubsection{Clock comparison}
Let $\pX_0=\pX(s_0)$ and $\pX_1=\pX(s_1)$ be fixed, time-like separated events.
Write $\pX_k=t_k+\br_k\cdot\bsig$ for $k=0,1$.
In their separation's rest frame, $\br_1=\br_0$ and
$t_1-t_0=\sqrt{\det(\pX_1-\pX_0)}$.  Since $\sqrt{1-\bv^2}\le1$,
\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~122}{page=130}
\begin{equation}
 s_1-s_0\le\sqrt{\det(\pX_1-\pX_0)}.
 \label{eq:maximum-proper-time}
\end{equation}
Equality requires the straight inertial path.  The determinant of a finite
displacement therefore does not generally equal the squared elapsed
proper time of an accelerated clock.

For a return trip lasting real coordinate time $T>0$, take velocities
$+\beta\bu$ and $-\beta\bu$ for $T/2$ each, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$ and
$0<\beta<1$.  The ideal instantaneous reversal is the limit of a short
smooth turn.  The traveling clock records
\[
 s(T)-s(0)=T\sqrt{1-\beta^2},
\]
while the clock at rest records $T$.  This is the twin-clock comparison
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~121}{page=127}.

\subsubsection{Constant proper acceleration}
Recall the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Let $a>0$ be a constant real proper-acceleration magnitude and $\bu$ a fixed
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis}.  Starting at $\pX(0)=0$ and $\pU(0)=1$, take rapidity
$\theta(s)=as$.  Then
\[
 \pU(s)=\pL(as,\bu)^{-2}=\cosh(as)+\sinh(as)\bu\cdot\bsig.
\]
Differentiation verifies the stated acceleration:
\[
 -\det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)
 =a^2\bigl(\cosh^2(as)-\sinh^2(as)\bigr)=a^2.
\]
Integrating \eqref{eq:worldline-integral} yields~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.33), pp.~142--143}{page=148}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pX(s)=\frac{\sinh(as)}{a}
       +\frac{\cosh(as)-1}{a}\bu\cdot\bsig.}
 \label{eq:hyperbolic-worldline}
\end{equation}
Its components give
\begin{align*}
 \bv(s)&=\frac{\dd\br/\dd s}{\dd t/\dd s}=\tanh(as)\bu,\\
 \bigl(1+a\bu\cdot\br\bigr)^2-(at)^2&=1.
\end{align*}
Thus \eqref{eq:hyperbolic-worldline} is the usual hyperbolic motion, with
speed below light speed at every finite $s$.

\subsubsection{Light cones}
For a real event separation $\Delta \pX=\Delta t+\Delta\br\cdot\bsig$,
the eigenvalues are $\Delta t\pm\lVert\Delta\br\rVert$.
Both are nonnegative precisely in the future causal cone:
\begin{equation}
 \Delta t\ge\lVert\Delta\br\rVert.
 \label{eq:future-light-cone}
\end{equation}
The boundary has $\det(\Delta \pX)=0$; for a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, its nonzero future rays are
\[
 \Delta \pX=\Delta t(1+\bu\cdot\bsig),
 \qquad \Delta t>0,\quad\bu^2=1.
\]
The Hermitian congruence~\eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} preserves
nonnegativity of the eigenvalues and the determinant, hence the cone and its
boundary.  For a future time-like or null path,
\[
 \lVert\Delta\br\rVert
 =\left\lVert\int\bv\dd t\right\rVert
 \le\int\lVert\bv\rVert\dd t\le\Delta t.
\]
Its endpoints therefore satisfy \eqref{eq:future-light-cone}; a space-like
separation cannot carry a causal signal
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~118}{page=124}.

\section{Inertial frames and particle dynamics}
\label{sec:inertial-frames}

Constant Lorentz transformations relate coordinates and measured quantities in
inertial frames. The invariant mass and proper time retain the meanings
established in Section~\ref{sec:kinematics}.

\subsection{Coordinates, intervals, and light cones}
\label{sec:boosts}

Recall $\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, with real rapidity $\theta$,
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$, $\beta=\tanh\theta$, and $\gamma=\cosh\theta$.
Here $\gamma$ is the factor for frame velocity $\beta\bu$.
The gamma form~\eqref{eq:boost-gamma-form} and its square
\eqref{eq:boost-square} are the same exponential at half and full rapidity.
For real $\pX=t+\br\cdot\bsig$, take coincident inertial origins at
$t=t'=0$.  Equation~\eqref{eq:general-lorentz-congruence} with $\pT=\pL$
gives $\pX'=\pL \pX \pL$: the primed frame moves at $+\beta\bu$.
Equations~\eqref{eq:boost-scalar-component}--\eqref{eq:boost-vector-component}
become~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.1)}{page=104}
\begin{align}
 t'&=\gamma(t-\beta\bu\cdot\br), \label{eq:lorentz-time}\\
 \br'&=\br_{\perp}+\gamma(\br_{\parallel}-\beta t\bu),
 \label{eq:lorentz-position}
\end{align}
where $\br_{\parallel}=(\bu\cdot\br)\bu$ and
$\br_{\perp}=\br-\br_{\parallel}$.
In particular, $\br=\beta t\bu$ maps to $\br'=0$, fixing the physical
meaning of the sign.  Reversing $\theta$ as in \eqref{eq:boost-inverse}
gives $\pX=\pL^{-1}\pX'\pL^{-1}$.



Specializing \eqref{eq:boost-null-components} to the real event $\pX$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~154}{page=160}
\begin{equation}
 t'\pm\bu\cdot\br'=e^{\mp\theta}(t\pm\bu\cdot\br).
 \label{eq:boost-null-coordinates}
\end{equation}
Multiplying the two signs in \eqref{eq:boost-null-coordinates} and using
$\br'_{\perp}=\br_{\perp}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 t'^2-\br'^2
 =(t'+\bu\cdot\br')(t'-\bu\cdot\br')-\br_{\perp}'^2
 =t^2-\br^2.
\end{equation*}
Thus the exponential stretches one light-cone coordinate and compresses
the other reciprocally.  The same identity holds for event differences;
in particular,
\begin{equation}
 \dd t'^2-\dd\br'^2=\det(\dd \pX')=\det(\dd \pX)
 =\dd t^2-\dd\br^2.
 \label{eq:boost-interval}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:boost-interval} preserves the interval~\eqref{eq:interval}
and light cones in every direction.  Future causal increments stay future
because $\dd t'=\gamma(\dd t-\beta\bu\cdot\dd\br)>0$ when
$\dd t>0$ and $\dd t\geq\lVert\dd\br\rVert$
\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~118}{page=124}.

In contrast, the squared norm is
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert \pX'\rVert^2
 =\gamma^2\bigl((t-\beta\bu\cdot\br)^2
 +(\bu\cdot\br-\beta t)^2\bigr)+\br_{\perp}^2.
\end{equation*}
It generally differs from $t^2+\br^2$.
The determinant controls proper time and causality; preserving this
squared norm instead would not give these Lorentz transformations.

\subsection{Spacetime increments and velocity}
Recall the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$,
along a real world line $\pX(s)$.  For constant $\pL$, differentiation gives
\begin{equation}
 \dd \pX'=\pL\dd \pX \pL.
 \label{eq:boost-worldline-step}
\end{equation}
Because $\dd s$ is invariant, $\pU'=\pL \pU \pL$.  At a point where
$\pU=\pL^{-2}=\gamma(1+\beta\bu\cdot\bsig)$, this gives $\pU'=1$ and
$\dd \pX'=\dd s$: the boost takes the particle's instantaneous tangent
to rest.  For varying velocity, a different $\pL$ is needed at each point;
Section~\ref{sec:varying-transformations} supplies its derivative terms.

Recall the particle velocity $\bv=\dd\br/\dd t$ and resolve it along
$\bu$.  Dividing the spatial increment by
$\dd t'=\gamma(1-\beta\bu\cdot\bv)\dd t$ gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eq.~(7.22)}{page=141}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bv'=\frac{\bv_{\perp}/\gamma+\bv_{\parallel}-\beta\bu}
                       {1-\beta\bu\cdot\bv}.}
 \label{eq:velocity-boost}
\end{equation}
Interval invariance also gives
\begin{equation}
 1-\bv'^2=\frac{1-\bv^2}{\gamma^2(1-\beta\bu\cdot\bv)^2}.
 \label{eq:velocity-causal-bound}
\end{equation}
Thus \eqref{eq:velocity-boost} preserves subluminal motion and light speed.
For two real proper times $s_1,s_2$, the constant-factor
law~\eqref{eq:boost-worldline-step} also integrates to
\begin{equation*}
 \pX'(s_2)-\pX'(s_1)
 =\pL\left(\int_{s_1}^{s_2}\pU(s)\dd s\right)\pL.
\end{equation*}
This is the transformed world-line integral~\eqref{eq:worldline-integral}.

\subsection{Clock rates and measured lengths}
For a clock at rest in the primed frame, \eqref{eq:lorentz-time} uses
$\dd\br=\beta\bu\dd t$, hence~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~119}{page=125}
\begin{equation}
 \dd t'=\gamma(1-\beta^2)\dd t=\dd t/\gamma.
 \label{eq:time-dilation}
\end{equation}
Here $\dd t'=\dd s$; finite elapsed time follows from
\eqref{eq:elapsed-proper-time} .

For a rod at rest in the unprimed frame, let $\Delta\br\in\R^3$ be its
fixed endpoint separation.  A simultaneous measurement in the primed
frame requires $\Delta t'=0$, so
$\Delta t=\beta\bu\cdot\Delta\br$.  Substituting in
\eqref{eq:lorentz-position} gives~\citeat{tong-relativity}{p.~123}{page=129}
\begin{equation}
 \Delta\br'_{\parallel}=\Delta\br_{\parallel}/\gamma.
 \label{eq:length-contraction}
\end{equation}
The transverse separation stays unchanged; thus a rod of any orientation has
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\Delta\br'\rVert^2
 =\lVert\Delta\br_{\perp}\rVert^2
 +\lVert\Delta\br_{\parallel}\rVert^2/\gamma^2.
\end{equation*}
Longitudinal contraction~\eqref{eq:length-contraction} therefore compares
endpoint events at equal primed times.
Equal unprimed times instead give
$\Delta t'=-\gamma\beta\bu\cdot\Delta\br$; simultaneity is frame-dependent.
The reciprocal statement follows by exchanging primes and reversing $\beta$.

\subsection{Composing frames}
For constant determinant-one factors $\pT,\pT'$, applying the frame change
$\pT$ and then $\pT'$ gives
\begin{equation*}
 \pZ''=\pT'\bigl(\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}\bigr)\pT'^{\mathsf H}
 =(\pT'\pT)\pZ(\pT'\pT)^{\mathsf H}.
\end{equation*}
Thus the combined factor is $\pT'\pT$: the rightmost factor acts first.

Collinear boosts add rapidities by \eqref{eq:boost-composition}.
For a second real rapidity $\theta'$, set $\beta'=\tanh\theta'$ and
$\gamma'=\cosh\theta'$.  Their combined parameters are
\begin{align*}
 \beta''&=\tanh(\theta+\theta')
 =\frac{\beta+\beta'}{1+\beta\beta'}, \\
 \gamma''&=\gamma\gamma'(1+\beta\beta').
\end{align*}
