These are the collinear velocity and clock-factor composition laws.
Noncollinear boosts instead have the rotation commutator
\eqref{eq:boost-generator-commutator}, producing the Thomas--Wigner rotation.

\subsection{Energy and momentum}
For the real momentum $\pP=E+\bp\cdot\bsig$, the same congruence gives~\citeat{feynman-spacetime}{Eq.~(17.12)}{Eq:I:17:12}
\begin{align}
 E'&=\gamma(E-\beta\bu\cdot\bp),
 \label{eq:energy-boost}\\
 \bp'&=\bp_{\perp}+\gamma(\bp_{\parallel}-\beta E\bu).
 \label{eq:momentum-boost}
\end{align}
Here the projections are along $\bu$.
Determinant invariance gives $E'^2-\bp'^2=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$ for a
particle of rest mass $m>0$.  To see the energy increase directly, start
with $\pP'=m$ in its rest frame and invert the boost:
\begin{equation*}
 \pP=\pL^{-1}m\pL^{-1}=m\gamma(1+\beta\bu\cdot\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Thus $E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\beta\bu$, while $m$ remains unchanged
\citeat{tong-relativity}{Eqs.~(7.25), (7.28)}{page=142}.
The force response is derived in
\eqref{eq:relativistic-inertia}--\eqref{eq:relativistic-work}.

\subsection{Free-particle action}

Recall real $m>0$, $\bv=\dd\br/\dd t$, and $\dd s=\dd t/\gamma$.
Here $L:\R\times\R^3\times\{\bv\in\R^3:\bv^2<1\}\to\R$
is the particle Lagrangian; the boost factor is $\pL\in\R\oplus\R^3$.
$\partial_{\bv}$ denotes its vector derivative with respect to the three
independent velocity components, while $\pdr$ denotes the spatial gradient.
The free action is the physical choice~\citeat{tong-em}{Eqs.~(5.38)--(5.39)}{page=130}
\begin{equation}
 \int L\dd t=-m\int\dd s.
 \label{eq:free-particle-action}
\end{equation}
Using $\dd s=\dd t/\gamma$ gives the Lagrangian
\begin{equation*}
 L=-m\sqrt{1-\bv^2}=-m/\gamma.
\end{equation*}
For endpoint-fixed real path variations $\delta\br(t)$, varying the
Lagrangian and integrating by parts gives
\[
 \delta\int L\dd t
 =\int\left(\pdr L-\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\partial_{\bv}L\right)
 \cdot\delta\br\dd t.
\]
Stationarity for every variation yields the Euler--Lagrange equation.
With Hamiltonian $H:=\bv\cdot\partial_{\bv}L-L\in\R$, the chain rule gives
\begin{align}
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\partial_{\bv}L&=\pdr L, \label{eq:euler-lagrange}\\
 \frac{\dd H}{\dd t}
 &=\bv\cdot\left(\frac{\dd}{\dd t}\partial_{\bv}L-\pdr L\right)
   -\partial_tL=-\partial_tL. \label{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance}
\end{align}
By \eqref{eq:euler-lagrange} and \eqref{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance},
spatial and temporal translation invariance conserve canonical
momentum and energy.  For the free action,
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{\bv}L&=m\gamma\bv=\bp,\\
 H&=m\gamma\bv^2+m/\gamma=m\gamma=E.
\end{align*}
Write $v=\lVert\bv\rVert\ll1$.  Here $O(v^n)$ denotes terms bounded
by a constant times $v^n$ as $v\to0$.  The binomial series gives
\begin{align*}
 \sqrt{1-v^2}&=1-\tfrac12v^2+O(v^4),\\
 \gamma&=1+\tfrac12v^2+O(v^4),\\
 L&=-m+\tfrac12mv^2+O(mv^4),\\
 H&=m+\tfrac12mv^2+O(mv^4).
\end{align*}
The exact kinetic energy above rest is
\begin{equation*}
 K:=H-m=m(\gamma-1)\in\R_{\ge0}.
\end{equation*}
This and both expansions recover $mv^2/2$
at low speed after removal of the constant rest term.

\section{Coordinates, frames, and differentiation}
\label{sec:differentiated-fields}

Fields have domain $\R^4$ (or an open coordinate region).
The symbols $\partial_t$, $\pdr$, $\pvpartial$, $\pvpartial^*$, and $\Box$
are differential operators. On a scalar field $f:\R^4\to\C$,
$\partial_t f,\Box f\in\C$, $\pdr f\in\C^3$, and
$\pvpartial f,\pvpartial^*f\in\C\oplus\C^3$ pointwise.
For real $f$, these outputs lie in the corresponding real spaces.
On a paravector field, $\pvpartial$ and $\pvpartial^*$ use algebra multiplication,
so even a real input may have a complex output.

\subsection{Paravector differential operators}

Let $t\in\R$ and $\br=\{r_1,r_2,r_3\}\in\R^3$ be real coordinates.
Define their scalar and vector derivatives by
\[
 \partial_t:=\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\qquad
 \pdr:=\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial r_1},
              \frac{\partial}{\partial r_2},
              \frac{\partial}{\partial r_3}\right\}.
\]
Define the partial paravector and its sigma conjugate by~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(176)}{page=29}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpartial:=\partial_t+\bsig\cdot\pdr
 =\partial_t+\sum_{n=1}^{3}\pvsigma_n\frac{\partial}{\partial r_n},
 \label{eq:paravector-derivative}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation*}
 \pvpartial^*=\adj{\pvpartial}=\partial_t-\bsig\cdot\pdr.
\end{equation*}
The sigma generators are constant.  Derivatives act from the left on
everything to their right unless parentheses specify their scope.
For fields with commuting second derivatives, anticommutation cancels
all mixed spatial terms:
\begin{align*}
 (\bsig\cdot\pdr)^2
 &=\sum_{n=1}^{3}\frac{\partial^2}{\partial r_n^2}
 +\sum_{n<m}(\pvsigma_n\pvsigma_m+\pvsigma_m\pvsigma_n)
           \frac{\partial^2}{\partial r_n\partial r_m}\\
 &=\pdr\cdot\pdr.
\end{align*}
The mixed time--space terms likewise cancel.  Both orders therefore give
the scalar d'Alembertian~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(178)}{page=30}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpartial\pvpartial^*=\pvpartial^*\pvpartial
 =\partial_t^2-\pdr\cdot\pdr=:\Box.
 \label{eq:dalembertian}
\end{equation}
These are operator identities: $\pvpartial\pvpartial^*\pZ$ means
$\pvpartial(\pvpartial^*\pZ)$.

\subsection{Components and the product rule}

Let $a,b:\R^4\to\R$ be real scalar functions and
$\bX,\bY:\R^4\to\R^3$ real vector functions.  They define the complex
paravector field
\[
 \pZ(t,\br)=(a+b\ii)+(\bX+\ii\bY)\cdot\bsig.
\]
Ordered vector multiplication gives the real and imaginary scalar and vector parts of $\pvpartial \pZ$:
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \pvpartial \pZ={}&(\partial_ta+\pdr\cdot\bX)
 +\ii(\partial_tb+\pdr\cdot\bY)\\
 &+(\pdr a+\partial_t\bX-\pdr\times\bY)\cdot\bsig\\
 &+\ii(\pdr b+\pdr\times\bX+\partial_t\bY)\cdot\bsig.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Multiplication by $\ii$ exchanges real and imaginary parts, with the
former imaginary parts acquiring a minus sign.

Here $\pZ:\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$. For a second field
$\pZ':\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$, the prime labels the
field rather than a derivative.  Apply the scalar product rule before
reordering any factors:
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial(\pZ \pZ')
 ={}&(\partial_t\pZ)\pZ'+\pZ(\partial_t\pZ')\\
 &+\sum_{n=1}^{3}\pvsigma_n
   \left(\frac{\partial \pZ}{\partial r_n}\pZ'
          +\pZ\frac{\partial \pZ'}{\partial r_n}\right)\\
 ={}&(\pvpartial \pZ)\pZ'+\pZ(\pvpartial \pZ')
    +\sum_{n=1}^{3}[\pvsigma_n,\pZ]\frac{\partial \pZ'}{\partial r_n}.
\end{align*}

\subsection{Flat spacetime in generalized coordinates}
\label{sec:generalized}

Here $(t,\br)$ denote generalized real chart coordinates.  Smooth functions $f:\R^4\to\R$ and $\bg:\R^4\to\R^3$,
restricted to a coordinate region, specify inertial coordinates
through $\pX\in\R\oplus\R^3$:
\[
 \pX=f(t,\br)+\bg(t,\br)\cdot\bsig.
\]
Along a future timelike path with proper time $s$, recall the real
$\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector}
with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Require the Jacobian of $(f,\bg)$ to be nonsingular.  Differentiating gives
\begin{align*}
 \dd \pX&=(\partial_tf+\partial_t\bg\cdot\bsig)\dd t
 +\sum_{k=1}^3(\partial_{r_k}f+\partial_{r_k}\bg\cdot\bsig)\dd r_k,\\
 \dd s^2&=\dd \pX\,\dd \pX^*=\dd f^2-\dd\bg^2.
\end{align*}
With $x_0=t$, $x_k=r_k$ and $\partial_k=\partial/\partial x_k$, the
interval is $\dd s^2=\sum_{j,k=0}^3 g_{jk}\dd x_j\dd x_k$, with
$x_j,g_{jk}\in\R$ and metric matrix $(g_{jk})\in\R^{4\times4}$:
\begin{equation*}
 g_{jk}=\partial_jf\,\partial_kf-\partial_j\bg\cdot\partial_k\bg,
 \qquad j,k=0,1,2,3.
\end{equation*}
This is the flat Minkowski metric in changed coordinates; varying
coefficients do not imply curvature.

Along this future timelike curve, $\dd f>0$.
Define its real inertial three-velocity $\bv=\dd\bg/\dd f$ and
$\gamma=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\bv^2}}$, with $\bv^2<1$.  Then
\begin{align*}
 \dd s&=\sqrt{1-\bv^2}\,\dd f,\\
 \pU=\frac{\dd \pX}{\dd s}&=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig).
\end{align*}
Apply \eqref{eq:proper-acceleration-components} with inertial time $f$
and coordinate acceleration $\dd\bv/\dd f$.
For inertial motion, the scalar part gives $\bv\cdot\dd\bv/\dd f=0$,
and its vector part then gives $\dd\bv/\dd f=0$.  Therefore
\begin{equation*}
 \bg=\bC_1f+\bC_2.
\end{equation*}
The real constants $\bC_1,\bC_2\in\R^3$ are inertial velocity and
position at inertial time $f=0$, with $\bC_1^2<1$.
Overdots denote total derivatives along this curve with respect to $t$;
wherever $\dot f\ne0$,
\begin{equation*}
 \dot{\bv}=\frac{\ddot{\bg}\dot f-\dot{\bg}\ddot f}{(\dot f)^2}.
\end{equation*}

\subsection{Varying frames and local bases}
\label{sec:varying-transformations}

Use a fixed coordinate chart and let the local frame vary along a curve.
Let $\pT(s)$ be a smooth complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector}
of \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{determinant one} along a real parameter $s$.  When $s$ is proper time,
recall the real tangent $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Here $\pT(s),\pZ(s)\in\C\oplus\C^3$. Define
$\pvOmega(s):=(\dd \pT/\dd s)\pT^{-1}\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$.
Its scalar part vanishes because
$\dd(\det \pT)/\dd s=2\scpart\pvOmega=0$.
The inverse rule gives
\[
 \frac{\dd \pT^{-1}}{\dd s}=-\pT^{-1}\pvOmega.
\]
For a complex paravector $\pZ(s)$ transformed by similarity,
$\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{-1}$, product differentiation yields
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}
 =\pT\frac{\dd \pZ}{\dd s}\pT^{-1}+[\pvOmega,\pZ'].
 \label{eq:varying-similarity}
\end{equation}
Differentiating again and replacing
$\pT(\dd \pZ/\dd s)\pT^{-1}$ by its first-derivative expression gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\dd^2 \pZ'}{\dd s^2}={}&\pT\frac{\dd^2\pZ}{\dd s^2}\pT^{-1}
 +2\left[\pvOmega,\frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}\right]\\
 &+\left[\frac{\dd\pvOmega}{\dd s},\pZ'\right]
 -[\pvOmega,[\pvOmega,\pZ']].
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
For a real paravector transformed instead by congruence,
$\pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H}$, the same rule gives
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd \pZ'}{\dd s}
 =\pT\frac{\dd \pZ}{\dd s}\pT^{\mathsf H}
 +\pvOmega \pZ'+\pZ'\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}.
 \label{eq:varying-congruence}
\end{equation}
Equations~\eqref{eq:varying-similarity} and \eqref{eq:varying-congruence}
include the frame terms omitted by a constant-factor transformation.
For a varying rotation $\pT=\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta(s)\bu(s)\cdot\bsig/2)$,
with real $\theta(s)$ and \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu(s)$, unitarity gives
$\pvOmega^{\mathsf H}=-\pvOmega$ and $\pvOmega\in\{0\}\oplus\ii\R^3$,
so both formulas agree.

When $s$ is proper time, the physical four-acceleration in the local frame is
$\pT(\dd \pU/\dd s)\pT^{\mathsf H}$.
In \eqref{eq:varying-congruence}, $\dd \pU'/\dd s$ also differentiates the
frame; the two $\pvOmega$ terms account for this change.  Likewise,
differentiating the transformed event $\pX'$ adds frame terms to its tangent.
The similarity formula also gives the local algebra-basis law
$\pvsigma_k'=\pT\pvsigma_k\pT^{-1}$, with
\begin{equation*}
 \frac{\dd\pvsigma_k'}{\dd s}=[\pvOmega,\pvsigma_k'],\qquad k=1,2,3.
\end{equation*}
This evolution preserves all generator products.  A general complex local basis
is nevertheless distinct from a real spacetime frame, whose vectors require
the congruence law.

\section{Electromagnetism}
\label{sec:electromagnetism}

All coefficient fields depend on real inertial coordinates $(t,\br)$.
Combine the electric and magnetic fields $\bE,\bBf:\R^4\to\R^3$
into the complex field vector $\bF:\R^4\to\C^3$ and its scalar-free
paravector $\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(23)}{page=19}:
\begin{equation}
 \bF:=\bE+\ii\bBf,\qquad \pF:=\bF\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:em-field-definition}
\end{equation}
Combine the charge density $\rho:\R^4\to\R$ and current density
$\bJ:\R^4\to\R^3$ into the real four-current paravector:
\[
 \pJ:=\rho+\bJ\cdot\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3,
 \qquad \pJ^*=\rho-\bJ\cdot\bsig.
\]
We use the paravector derivative of \eqref{eq:paravector-derivative},
with spatial gradient $\pdr$ and d'Alembertian
$\Box=\pvpartial^*\pvpartial=\partial_t^2-\pdr^2$.
The symbols $\pvpartial$, $\pvpartial^*$, and $\Box$ denote differential operators.

The dot/cross product~\eqref{eq:vector-product} combines metric and
orientation. With a spatial derivative it gives divergence and curl,
packing the six real electromagnetic components into one complex field $\pF$.
The product explains the compact form; Maxwell dynamics remains physical
input, derived from an action in Section~\ref{sec:maxwell-action}.

\subsection{Maxwell's equations and wave propagation}

Maxwell's four equations are the single field law~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(177)}{page=29}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pvpartial \pF=\pJ^*.}
 \label{eq:maxwell}
\end{equation}
The product rule expands its left side into
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial \pF={}&\underbrace{\pdr\cdot\bE}_{\text{real scalar}}
 +\underbrace{\ii\pdr\cdot\bBf}_{\text{imaginary scalar}}\\
 &+\underbrace{(\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf)\cdot\bsig}_{\text{real vector}}\\
 &+\underbrace{\ii(\partial_t\bBf+\pdr\times\bE)\cdot\bsig}_{\text{imaginary vector}}.
\end{align*}
Equating the four parts gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eqs.~(1.4)--(1.7)}{page=14}:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\pdr\cdot\bE=\rho,
 \label{eq:gauss-electric}\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &\pdr\cdot\bBf=0,
 \label{eq:gauss-magnetic}\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf=-\bJ,
 \label{eq:ampere-maxwell}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\partial_t\bBf+\pdr\times\bE=0.
 \label{eq:faraday}
\end{align}
These are Gauss' electric and magnetic laws, Amp\`ere--Maxwell's law,
and Faraday's law.
Applying $\pvpartial^*$ gives the sourced wave equation:
\[
 \pvpartial^*(\pvpartial \pF)=\Box \pF=\pvpartial^*\pJ^*.
\]
Taking its star yields~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(152)}{page=26}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\Box \pF^*=\pvpartial \pJ.}
 \label{eq:maxwell-wave}
\end{equation}
Expanding the source derivative gives
\[
 \pvpartial \pJ
 =(\partial_t\rho+\pdr\cdot\bJ)
 +(\partial_t\bJ+\pdr\rho+\ii\pdr\times\bJ)\cdot\bsig.
\]
Because $\pF$ is scalar-free, its real and imaginary scalar parts vanish.
Matching all four parts gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(1.1)}{page=12}:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &0=\partial_t\rho+\pdr\cdot\bJ,
 \label{eq:charge-continuity}\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\notag\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bE=-\partial_t\bJ-\pdr\rho,
 \label{eq:electric-wave}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\Box\bBf=\pdr\times\bJ.
 \label{eq:magnetic-wave}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:charge-continuity} is charge conservation;
\eqref{eq:electric-wave} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-wave} are the sourced field waves.
In source-free vacuum, $\pJ=0$ and $\Box \pF=0$: both fields obey the free wave equation.

\subsection{Potentials and gauge freedom}

Combine the scalar potential $V:\R^4\to\R$ and vector potential
$\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$ into the real four-potential, and define its four-divergence:
\[
 \pA:=V+\bA\cdot\bsig\in\R\oplus\R^3,
 \qquad S:=\scpart{\pvpartial \pA}\in\R.
\]
The product rule gives
\begin{align*}
 \pvpartial \pA={}&(\partial_tV+\pdr\cdot\bA)
 +(\partial_t\bA+\pdr V)\cdot\bsig
 +\ii(\pdr\times\bA)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
Subtracting its scalar part gives the field~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~105}{page=113}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pF^*=\pvpartial \pA-S.}
 \label{eq:potential-field}
\end{equation}
Thus $\pvpartial \pA=S+\pF^*\in\R\oplus\C^3$, with components:
\begin{align}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &S=\partial_tV+\pdr\cdot\bA,\notag\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\notag\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\bE=-\partial_t\bA-\pdr V,
 \label{eq:electric-potential}\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\bBf=\pdr\times\bA.
 \label{eq:magnetic-potential}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:potential-field} separates the four-divergence from the field;
\eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential} satisfy
\eqref{eq:gauss-magnetic} and \eqref{eq:faraday} identically.

Applying $\pvpartial^*$ gives the source equations in any gauge:
\[
 \Box \pA=\pvpartial^*(\pvpartial \pA)
 =\pvpartial^*(S+\pF^*)=\pvpartial^*S+(\pvpartial \pF)^*.
\]
Using Maxwell's equation~\eqref{eq:maxwell} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.1)}{page=142}:
\[
 \boxed{\Box \pA=\pvpartial^*S+\pJ.}
\]
Separating scalar and vector parts gives
\begin{align*}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\Box V-\partial_tS=\rho,\\
 \text{Imaginary scalar:}\quad &0=0,\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bA+\pdr S=\bJ,\\
 \text{Imaginary vector:}\quad &\bm 0=\bm 0.
\end{align*}
These source equations hold before gauge fixing.
For a real gauge function $\lambda:\R^4\to\R$, let~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.2)}{page=142}:
\[
 \pA':=\pA+\pvpartial^*\lambda.
\]
Differentiating gives $\pvpartial \pA'=\pvpartial \pA+\Box\lambda$.
Only the scalar changes, so the field is gauge invariant:
\[
 S'=S+\Box\lambda,\qquad \pF'=\pF.
\]
In the Lorenz gauge $S=0$, the potential wave equations are~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(6.4)}{page=143}:
\begin{align*}
 \text{Real scalar:}\quad &\Box V=\rho,\\
 \text{Real vector:}\quad &\Box\bA=\bJ.
\end{align*}

\subsection{The electromagnetic field action}
\label{sec:maxwell-action}

For real scalar potential $V(t,\br)$ and vector potential
$\bA(t,\br)$, write $\pA=V+\bA\cdot\bsig$.  With prescribed conserved
current $\pJ=\rho+\bJ\cdot\bsig$,
Maxwell dynamics follows from the scalar Lagrangian density
$L:\R^4\to\R$~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.42)}{page=133}\,\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.43)}{page=134}
\begin{equation}
 L=\tfrac12\repart{\pF^2}-\scpart{\pA^*\pJ}
  =\tfrac12(\bE^2-\bBf^2)-\rho V+\bJ\cdot\bA.
 \label{eq:maxwell-lagrangian}
\end{equation}
For boundary-vanishing variations $\delta V\in\R$ and $\delta\bA\in\R^3$,
the induced field variations $\delta\bE,\delta\bBf\in\R^3$ satisfy
\begin{align*}
 \delta\bE&=-\pdr\delta V-\partial_t\delta\bA,\\
 \delta\bBf&=\pdr\times\delta\bA.
\end{align*}
Integration by parts gives
\begin{equation*}
 \delta\int L\dd t\dd^3\br
 =\int\left((\pdr\cdot\bE-\rho)\delta V
 +(\partial_t\bE-\pdr\times\bBf+\bJ)\cdot\delta\bA\right)\dd t\dd^3\br.
\end{equation*}
Independent variations give
\eqref{eq:gauss-electric} and \eqref{eq:ampere-maxwell}; the potential
identities supply \eqref{eq:gauss-magnetic} and \eqref{eq:faraday}.
Together they recover \eqref{eq:maxwell}.

\subsection{Field invariants}

Since $\pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3$, its adjugate is $-\pF$.
Its square is a complex scalar, $\pF^2\in\C$~\citeat{baylis-applications}{p.~106}{page=117}
\begin{equation}
 \pF^2=-\det \pF=\bE^2-\bBf^2+2\ii\bE\cdot\bBf.
 \label{eq:field-invariants}
\end{equation}
For a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$, the field similarity
$\pF'=\pT \pF \pT^{-1}$ preserves this scalar by \eqref{eq:det-similarity}.
Physical rotations and boosts use
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ and
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, respectively, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$
and real angle or rapidity $\theta$; their field laws are derived in
Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.
The real and imaginary parts of \eqref{eq:field-invariants} are
therefore observer-independent.
At a point with a nonzero field, they give the classification
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Condition & Consequence\\
\midrule
$\bE\cdot\bBf=0$, $\bE^2>\bBf^2$ & Some frame has $\bBf'=0$.\\
$\bE\cdot\bBf=0$, $\bE^2<\bBf^2$ & Some frame has $\bE'=0$.\\
$\bE\cdot\bBf\ne0$ & Neither field can be eliminated.\\
$\bE\cdot\bBf=0$, $\bE^2=\bBf^2$ & Null field: equal, perpendicular fields.\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the first two rows, choose respectively
\[
 \tanh\theta\,\bu=\frac{\bE\times\bBf}{\bE^2},
 \qquad
 \tanh\theta\,\bu=\frac{\bE\times\bBf}{\bBf^2}.
\]
Both speeds are less than one.  Substitution in
\eqref{eq:electric-boost} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-boost} gives
$\bBf'=0$ and $\bE'=0$, respectively.

\subsection{Lorentz force from the action}

Recall the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s=\gamma(1+\bv\cdot\bsig)$,
an \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$.
Let $q\in\R$ be charge and $m>0$ constant mass.  The real momentum is
$\pP=m\pU=E+\bp\cdot\bsig$, with $E=m\gamma$ and $\bp=m\gamma\bv$.
The real particle Lagrangian $L$ is fixed by the action~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.41)}{page=132}
\begin{equation}
 \int L\dd t=-m\int\dd s-q\int\scpart{\pA^*\pU}\dd s.
 \label{eq:charged-particle-action}
\end{equation}
Since $\scpart{\pA^*\pU}=\gamma(V-\bv\cdot\bA)$, the Lagrangian is
\begin{equation*}
 L=-m/\gamma-qV+q\bv\cdot\bA.
\end{equation*}
Its Euler--Lagrange terms are
\begin{align*}
 \partial_{\bv}L&=\bp+q\bA,\\
 \frac{\dd}{\dd t}\partial_{\bv}L
 &=\frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}+q\partial_t\bA+q(\bv\cdot\pdr)\bA,\\
 \pdr L&=-q\pdr V+q\pdr(\bv\cdot\bA).
\end{align*}
Here spatial differentiation holds the independent velocity argument fixed.
Using
$\pdr(\bv\cdot\bA)-(\bv\cdot\pdr)\bA
=\bv\times(\pdr\times\bA)$ gives~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.32)}{page=124}
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd\bp}{\dd t}=q(\bE+\bv\times\bBf).
 \label{eq:lorentz-momentum}
\end{equation}
Here \eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential}
turn the potential derivatives into the fields.
The Hamiltonian is
\begin{equation}
 H=\bv\cdot(\bp+q\bA)-L=E+qV.
 \label{eq:charged-hamiltonian}
\end{equation}
Using \eqref{eq:hamiltonian-energy-balance} in \eqref{eq:charged-hamiltonian},
\begin{align*}
 \frac{\dd E}{\dd t}+q\partial_tV+q\bv\cdot\pdr V
 &=-\partial_tL=q\partial_tV-q\bv\cdot\partial_t\bA.
\end{align*}
Canceling $q\partial_tV$ and using \eqref{eq:electric-potential} gives~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~116}{page=124}
\begin{equation}
 \frac{\dd E}{\dd t}=q\bv\cdot\bE.
 \label{eq:lorentz-energy}
\end{equation}
This is the mechanical work rate. Thus $\bp+q\bA$ and $H=E+qV$ are
canonical momentum and energy. The momentum $\bp$ is kinetic (also called
mechanical); the particle energy $E$ includes rest energy and excludes
the potential energy $qV$.
Canonical energy is conserved for static potentials in the chosen gauge.
A time-dependent gauge transformation can change $H$ while leaving $E$
and the force unchanged.

Now expand the complex product of the field and four-velocity:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \frac{\pF \pU}{\gamma}={}&\bv\cdot\bE
   +(\bE+\bv\times\bBf)\cdot\bsig\\
 &+\ii\left(\bv\cdot\bBf
   +(\bBf-\bv\times\bE)\cdot\bsig\right).
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:force-product}
\end{equation}
The real scalar and vector parts reproduce
\eqref{eq:lorentz-energy} and \eqref{eq:lorentz-momentum}.
Since $\pP$ is real and $\dd/\dd s=\gamma\dd/\dd t$, they combine into~\citeat{baylis-charge}{Eq.~(2)}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\frac{\dd \pP}{\dd s}
 =q\repart{\pF \pU}=\frac q2(\pF \pU+\pU \pF^{\mathsf H}).}
 \label{eq:lorentz}
\end{equation}
Hermitian extraction enforces real kinetic
four-momentum and reproduces the action-derived force.  The imaginary part
need not vanish for an electric charge.

\subsection{Acceleration and the mass shell}
\label{sec:lorentz-proper-acceleration}

Recall the real four-velocity $\pU=\dd \pX/\dd s$, an
\hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} with $\pU \pU^{-1}=1$ and
$\pU^{-1}=\pU^*$, real momentum $\pP=m\pU$, and coordinate acceleration
$\ba=\dd\bv/\dd t$.  Scalar-part cyclicity proves tangency to the mass shell:
\begin{align*}
 \scpart{\pU^*\dd \pP/\dd s}
 &=\frac q2\scpart{\pU^*\pF \pU+\pF^{\mathsf H}}\\
 &=\frac q2\left(\scpart {\pF}+\scpart{\pF^{\mathsf H}}\right)=0.
\end{align*}
This agrees with $\scpart{\pU^*\dd \pU/\dd s}=0$ in
Section~\ref{sec:proper-acceleration}.
Combining \eqref{eq:proper-acceleration-components} with the force components
in \eqref{eq:force-product} gives
\begin{align*}
 m\gamma^3\bv\cdot\ba&=q\bv\cdot\bE,\\
 m\gamma\left(\ba+\gamma^2(\bv\cdot\ba)\bv\right)
 &=q(\bE+\bv\times\bBf).
\end{align*}
Eliminating $\bv\cdot\ba$ yields the coordinate acceleration
\begin{equation*}
 \boxed{\ba=\frac{q}{m\gamma}
 \left(\bE+\bv\times\bBf-(\bv\cdot\bE)\bv\right).}
\end{equation*}
The magnetic term changes momentum without doing work.
Taking the determinant of the real force gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 -\det\left(\frac{\dd \pP}{\dd s}\right)
 &=-m^2\det\left(\frac{\dd \pU}{\dd s}\right)\\
 &=q^2\gamma^2\left((\bE+\bv\times\bBf)^2-(\bv\cdot\bE)^2\right).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Dividing by $m^2$ gives the invariant squared proper acceleration.
The factor $\gamma^2((\bE+\bv\times\bBf)^2-(\bv\cdot\bE)^2)$
is the squared electric field in the particle's instantaneous rest frame.

\subsection{Energy and momentum flux}

Recall $\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\cdot\bsig$.  Its Hermitian product is~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(180)}{page=30}
\begin{equation*}
 \tfrac12\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}
 =\tfrac12(\bE^2+\bBf^2)+(\bE\times\bBf)\cdot\bsig.
\end{equation*}
Define $u=(\bE^2+\bBf^2)/2\in\R_{\ge0}$ and the Poynting
vector $\bS=\bE\times\bBf\in\R^3$.  These coefficients are frame-dependent,
whereas $\pF^2$ contains the Lorentz invariants.
The full stress--energy tensor is represented by the real linear map~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(167)}{page=28}
\begin{equation}
 T(\pN):=\tfrac12\pF \pN \pF^{\mathsf H},
 \label{eq:electromagnetic-stress-map}
\end{equation}
Here $T:\R\oplus\R^3\to\R\oplus\R^3$ is a real-linear map, with
$\pN\in\R\oplus\R^3$; $T$ denotes the stress map, not a frame factor.  Its time-direction value is
$T(1)=u+\bS\cdot\bsig$.  It is future causal because $u\geq0$ and
\begin{equation*}
 \det(T(1))=u^2-\bS^2=\tfrac14\lVert\det \pF\rVert^2\geq0.
\end{equation*}
Equality holds for null fields.  Reversing the order gives
$\pF^{\mathsf H}\pF=(\pF \pF^{\mathsf H})^*$: the same energy density and opposite
flux.  The transformation of the full map is derived in
Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.

Using Maxwell's vector equations,
\begin{align*}
 \partial_tu
 &=\bE\cdot(\pdr\times\bBf-\bJ)-\bBf\cdot(\pdr\times\bE)\\
 &=-\pdr\cdot\bS-\bJ\cdot\bE,
\end{align*}
where
$\pdr\cdot(\bE\times\bBf)
=\bBf\cdot(\pdr\times\bE)-\bE\cdot(\pdr\times\bBf)$.
Thus Poynting's energy-balance law is~\citeat{tong-em}{Eq.~(5.45)}{page=136}
\begin{equation}
 \partial_tu+\pdr\cdot\bS=-\bJ\cdot\bE.
 \label{eq:poynting}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, the left side is
$\tfrac12\partial_t\scpart{\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}}
+\tfrac12\pdr\cdot\vecpart{\pF \pF^{\mathsf H}}$.
The term $\bJ\cdot\bE$ is the power delivered to matter per unit
volume.

\section{Quantum amplitudes and differential operators}
\label{sec:waves}

Here $\pX=t+\br\cdot\bsig$ has real coordinates $(t,\br)\in\R^4$,
$m>0$ is mass, $q\in\R$ is charge, and $\hbar=c=1$.
Amplitudes are complex functions of these real coordinates;
their scalar or paravector type is specified below.

\subsection{Spinor amplitudes and probability density}
\label{sec:quantum-amplitudes}
\label{sec:paravector-inner-products}
\label{sec:axis-ideal-basis}

All products and norms here act on paravector values at one real event
$\pX$.  They sum algebra components only; no spatial integration is implied.
For $\pvvarphi,\pvpsi\in\C\oplus\C^3$, their algebraic inner product is
$\langle\pvvarphi\mid\pvpsi\rangle:=\scpart{\pvvarphi^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi}\in\C$.
Two entries separated by a vertical bar denote this inner product;
one entry in angle brackets denotes a normalized expectation below.

For arbitrary constant perpendicular \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vectors}
$\bu,\ba$, choose a constant complex paravector $\pvpsi_+$ of unit norm
with $(\bu\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_+=\pvpsi_+$, and set $\pvpsi_-=(\ba\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_+$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 (\bu\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm=\pm\pvpsi_\pm.
 \label{eq:axis-ideal-basis}
\end{equation}
The pair is orthonormal: $\scpart{\pvpsi_a^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_b}=\delta_{ab}$
for $a,b=+,-$.  A Pauli amplitude or either Dirac entry is~\citeat{baylis-spin}{Eq.~(69a)}{page=13}
\begin{equation}
 \pvpsi(t,\br)=c_+(t,\br)\pvpsi_++c_-(t,\br)\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:axis-ideal-components}
\end{equation}
Here $c_+,c_-:\R^4\to\C$, and pointwise
\[
 \pvpsi\in\mathrm{span}_{\C}\{\pvpsi_+,\pvpsi_-\}\subset\C\oplus\C^3.
\]
This fixed two-dimensional complex subspace is a left ideal: left
multiplication by any paravector preserves it. It is represented by $\C^2$,
not the full four-complex-dimensional paravector space.
Here $\pvpsi_\pm$ denote constant basis elements; in the Dirac sections they
denote fields taking values in this same ideal.
Left multiplication by $\bu\cdot\bsig$ or $\ba\cdot\bsig$ preserves
this two-component form; these generate all paravector left actions.
The matrix verification is in Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-spinors}.

The local squared norm $\rho:\R^4\to\R_{\ge0}$ is~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(1.1a)}{page=1}
\begin{equation}
 \rho:=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi}\geq0.
 \label{eq:probability-density}
\end{equation}
This $\rho$ denotes probability density, distinct from charge density
in the electromagnetic sections.
Orthonormality gives $\rho=\lVert c_+\rVert^2+\lVert c_-\rVert^2$.
For a Pauli wavefunction normalized to unit total probability, this is
its position density; Dirac uses the sum over its two entries.
Total spatial normalization is separate from the pointwise norm.
This probability interpretation does not apply to an arbitrary scalar KG field.

\subsection{Weighted densities and local expectations}
Let $\pZ=c+\bZ\cdot\bsig$ be real ($\pZ^{\mathsf H}=\pZ$),
with $c\in\R$ and $\bZ\in\R^3$.
It acts by multiplication, not differentiation.
For $\bZ\ne0$, take the real unit vector $\bu=\bZ/\lVert\bZ\rVert$
and recall the projectors $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig)/2$ from
Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors}.  Their spectral decomposition gives
\begin{equation}
 \scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}
 =(c+\lVert\bZ\rVert)\lVert\pvPi_+\pvpsi\rVert^2
 +(c-\lVert\bZ\rVert)\lVert\pvPi_-\pvpsi\rVert^2.
 \label{eq:weighted-density-spectral}
\end{equation}
For $\bZ=0$, this reduces to $c\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2$.
Thus the product is nonnegative for every $\pvpsi$ exactly when
$c\geq\lVert\bZ\rVert$.  For such a \emph{nonnegative weight}, define
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2
:=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}
 =\lVert\sqrt {\pZ}\,\pvpsi\rVert^2\geq0.}
 \label{eq:weighted-density}
\end{equation}
The square root has nonnegative eigenvalues, as in Section~\ref{sec:spectral}.
For $\pZ=1$, this is $\rho$.  It is a true squared norm when
$c>\lVert\bZ\rVert$; at equality it can vanish for nonzero $\pvpsi$,
so the notation then denotes a weighted density.  Projectors are an
important example.  Reality alone is insufficient: $\pZ=-1$ gives
$\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}=-\rho$.

\paragraph{Local expectations.}
For $\rho>0$ and $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$ acting by multiplication,
define the scalar expectation $\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi\in\C$~\citeat{sparks-quantum}{Eq.~(6.1)}{page=49}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi
:=\frac{\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pZ\pvpsi}}{\rho}.}
 \label{eq:paravector-expectation}
\end{equation}
For real $\pZ$, this is real and may have either sign.
Dividing \eqref{eq:weighted-density-spectral} by $\rho$ shows its meaning:
it averages the two eigenvalues with their projected probabilities.
For fields this mean is conditional on position; it is not a spatial
average.  A whole-wavefunction expectation additionally requires spatial
weighting.  The same formula extends linearly to complex $\pZ$, including
commutators.  For a nonnegative weight,
$\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_\pZ^2/\rho$.
For a projector, this ratio is a Born probability.

\paragraph{Transforming the amplitude.}
For a constant complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector} $\pT$,
set $\pvpsi'=\pT\pvpsi$.  For a nonnegative weight $\pZ$, substitution gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\lVert\pvpsi'\rVert_\pZ^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT\pvpsi}
 =\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT}^2.}
 \label{eq:weighted-density-pullback}
\end{equation}
This also proves that the transformed weight is nonnegative.
The same substitution in \eqref{eq:paravector-expectation} applies to
any complex $\pZ$.  With $\rho'=\lVert\pvpsi'\rVert^2>0$, it gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}
 =\frac{\rho}{\rho'}
   \langle \pT^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pT\rangle_\pvpsi.}
 \label{eq:amplitude-pullback}
\end{equation}
For $\pT=\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, with real angle $\theta$
and real unit $\bu$, unitarity preserves both
$\rho'=\rho$ and $\langle\pvvarphi'\mid\pvpsi'\rangle=\langle\pvvarphi\mid\pvpsi\rangle$.
For $\pT=\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, with real rapidity $\theta$,
this one-sided law instead gives
$\rho'=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pL^2}^2$; the norm and inner product
are generally not invariant.  The two Dirac entries use opposite boost
factors, as derived in Section~\ref{sec:transformations}.

\Needspace{10\baselineskip}
\subsection{Energy and momentum as differential operators}
\label{sec:quantum-energy-momentum}

Recall $\pX=t+\br\cdot\bsig$, with real coordinates $(t,\br)\in\R^4$.
Temporarily restore Planck's constant $h>0$ and $\hbar=h/(2\pi)$,
keeping $c=1$.  For a free wave, let $\omega\in\R$ be angular frequency
($2\pi$ times cycles per unit time), and let $\bk\in\R^3$ be its
wavenumber vector in cycles per unit length.  For $\bk\ne0$, the wavelength
$\lambda>0$ satisfies $\lVert\bk\rVert=1/\lambda$.

The physical input is the Planck--Einstein energy relation and de Broglie
momentum relation~\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~248}{page=5}\,\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~249}{page=6}:
\begin{align}
 E&=\hbar\omega,
 \label{eq:planck-einstein}\\
 \bp&=h\bk=2\pi\hbar\bk.
 \label{eq:debroglie-momentum}
\end{align}
Here $E\in\R$ is total energy, including rest energy, and
$\bp\in\R^3$ is momentum; its magnitude is $h/\lambda$.
Photoelectric measurements test the energy relation~\citeat{millikan1924}{p.~62}{page=9};
electron diffraction tests the momentum relation~\citeat{debroglie1929}{p.~255}{page=12}.

For the constant real four-momentum $\pP=E+\bp\cdot\bsig$, the
complex scalar plane wave is
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 f(\pX,\pP)&:=\exp\!\left(-\ii(\omega t-2\pi\bk\cdot\br)\right)\\
       &=\exp\!\left(-\frac{\ii}{\hbar}(Et-\bp\cdot\br)\right).
 \end{aligned}
\]
The second line substitutes \eqref{eq:planck-einstein} and
\eqref{eq:debroglie-momentum}.  Differentiating gives
\begin{align*}
 \ii\hbar\partial_t f&=Ef,\\
 -\ii\hbar\pdr f&=\bp f.
\end{align*}
Thus $\ii\hbar\partial_t$ and $-\ii\hbar\pdr$ represent energy and
momentum on each mode, and extend to superpositions by linearity.
Returning to $\hbar=1$ means $h=2\pi$, giving~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.104)}{page=106}
\begin{equation}
 f(\pX,\pP)=\exp\!\left(-\ii(Et-\bp\cdot\br)\right).
 \label{eq:plane-wave}
\end{equation}
The two eigenvalue equations combine into
\begin{equation}
 \ii\pvpartial^*f=\pP f.
 \label{eq:momentum-operator-eigenvalues}
\end{equation}

Define the canonical energy and momentum operators by
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \hE&:=\ii\partial_t,\\
 \hbp&:=-\ii\pdr,
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:canonical-operators}
\end{equation}
which combine to form the canonical four-momentum operator:
\begin{equation}
 \hP:=\ii\pvpartial^*=\hE+\hbp\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:canonical-paravector}
\end{equation}

Here $\hE$ is a scalar differential operator, $\hbp$ is a triple of
scalar differential operators, and $\hP$ is a paravector differential
operator. These act on fields, not elements of the numerical coefficient
spaces. On a smooth scalar field $\psi$, the pointwise outputs satisfy
$\hE\psi\in\C$, $\hbp\psi\in\C^3$, and
$\hP\psi\in\C\oplus\C^3$.
On spinor fields, $\hP$ preserves the fixed ideal of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}. The coupled operators have the same types.

In a field, recall the real potential paravector
$\pA(t,\br)=V(t,\br)+\bA(t,\br)\cdot\bsig$:
$V:\R^4\to\R$, $\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$, and
$\pA:\R^4\to\R\oplus\R^3$.
Within the coupling formulas, subscript $0$ labels the uncoupled
operators of \eqref{eq:canonical-operators}:
\begin{align*}
 \hE_0&:=\ii\partial_t,\\
 \hbp_0&:=-\ii\pdr,\\
 \hP_0&:=\ii\pvpartial^*.
\end{align*}
The charge is $q\in\R$.  The particle action gives canonical energy
$E+qV$ and momentum $\bp+q\bA$; identifying them with translation
operators defines the gauge-covariant energy and kinetic momentum operators~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eqs.~(2.11)--(2.12)}{page=6}:
\begin{align}
 \hEq&:=\hE_0-qV,
 \label{eq:mechanical-energy-operator}\\
 \hbpq&:=\hbp_0-q\bA,
 \label{eq:mechanical-momentum-operator}
\end{align}
which combine to form the kinetic four-momentum operator:
\begin{equation}
 \hPq:=\hP_0-q\pA=\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:mechanical-paravector}
\end{equation}
Here $\hEq$ includes rest energy and excludes the potential energy $qV$.
Hats distinguish named differential
operators from physical values and mode eigenvalues $E,\bp$.
The derivative symbols $\pvpartial$, $\pdr$, and $\Box$ remain unhatted.
Fields obtained by differentiating potentials are coefficient fields,
not differential operators. The operators act to the right.
The adjugate of $\hPq$ reverses the vector part without conjugating $\ii$.
Operator order matters in a field; an ordinary paravector determinant
cannot replace their product.  The canonical operators commute, giving
\begin{equation}
 \hP\,\adj\hP=\det\hP=\hE^2-\hbp^2=-\Box.
 \label{eq:free-momentum-product}
\end{equation}
Reversing the order gives the same result.

\subsection{Field strength from noncommuting derivatives}
Recall the real electric and magnetic fields from
\eqref{eq:electric-potential} and \eqref{eq:magnetic-potential}, and
$S=\partial_tV+\pdr\cdot\bA$, the real scalar in
\eqref{eq:potential-field}.  The real potential is $\pA=V+\bA\cdot\bsig$;
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\cdot\bsig$ is a complex vector paravector.

Apply the operators to a differentiable complex amplitude.
The product rule cancels all derivatives of the amplitude in the
commutators; writing $\hat P_{q,j}:=(\hbpq)_j$ for Cartesian components
$j,k=1,2,3$,
\begin{align*}
 [\hEq,\hat P_{q,j}]&=-\ii q(\partial_t\pA_j+\partial_jV)=\ii qE_j,\\
 [\hat P_{q,j},\hat P_{q,k}]&=\ii q(\partial_j\pA_k-\partial_k\pA_j),\\
 \hbpq\times\hbpq&=\ii q\bBf.
\end{align*}
Sigma multiplication and the energy commutator consequently give~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.13)}{page=6}\,\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{p.~6}{page=6}
\begin{equation}
 (\hbpq\cdot\bsig)^2=\hbpq^2-q\bBf\cdot\bsig,
 \label{eq:kinetic-sigma-square}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
 [\hEq,\hbpq\cdot\bsig]=\ii q\bE\cdot\bsig.
 \label{eq:kinetic-electric-commutator}
\end{equation}
Here $E_j$ is an electric-field component. Reversing the commutator
order and combining the electric and magnetic terms also gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 [\hbpq\cdot\bsig,\hEq]&=-\ii q\bE\cdot\bsig,\\
 \hbpq^2+\ii q\pF
 &=(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)^2-[\hbpq\cdot\bsig,\hEq].
 \end{aligned}
\]
Using \eqref{eq:kinetic-sigma-square} and
\eqref{eq:kinetic-electric-commutator} in the ordered products gives
\begin{align}
 (\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig)(\hEq-\hbpq\cdot\bsig)
 &=\hEq^2-(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)^2-[\hEq,\hbpq\cdot\bsig]\notag\\
 &=\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-\ii q\pF,
 \label{eq:covariant-product-plus}\\[0.35em]
 (\hEq-\hbpq\cdot\bsig)(\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig)
 &=\hEq^2-(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)^2+[\hEq,\hbpq\cdot\bsig]\notag\\
 &=\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-\ii q\pF^*.
 \label{eq:covariant-product-minus}
\end{align}
Their common scalar part gives KG; the vector terms give the Dirac
spin coupling.


\section{The Klein--Gordon equation}
\label{sec:klein-gordon-development}

\subsection{The mass shell as a wave equation}

Let $\psi:\R^4\to\C$ be a scalar field of constant mass $m\in\R_{>0}$.
The spacetime derivation in Section~\ref{sec:kinematics} gives the real
momentum $\pP=m\pU\in\R\oplus\R^3$ and its mass shell
$\det \pP=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$.
Represent $E\in\R$ and $\bp\in\R^3$ by the translation operators
$\hE=\ii\partial_t$ and $\hbp=-\ii\pdr$ from
\eqref{eq:canonical-operators}. Imposing the mass shell on the scalar
wavefunction gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=(\det\hP-m^2)\psi\\
  &=(\hE^2-\hbp^2-m^2)\psi\\
  &=(-\partial_t^2+\pdr^2-m^2)\psi\\
  &=-(\Box+m^2)\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Here the free coefficients commute, so the operator determinant is
the scalar operator in \eqref{eq:free-momentum-product}.
This yields the Klein--Gordon equation~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(1.13)}{page=15}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{(\Box+m^2)\psi=0.}
 \label{eq:kg}
\end{equation}

\subsection{Positive and negative frequency}
Recall the complex scalar field $\psi(t,\br)$ and real mass $m>0$.
Here the branch fields $\psi_\pm:\R^4\to\C$ are scalars, not Dirac entries.
Define the positive scalar operator
\[
 \hOmega:=\sqrt{m^2-\pdr^2},\qquad
 \hOmega^2=\det(m+\pdr\cdot\bsig).
\]
Since $\hOmega$ commutes with $\partial_t$, KG factors as
\begin{equation*}
 \Box+m^2=\partial_t^2+\hOmega^2
        =(\partial_t-\ii\hOmega)(\partial_t+\ii\hOmega).
\end{equation*}
The reverse product is the same.  Its two frequency branches obey
\[
 (\partial_t\pm\ii\hOmega)\psi_\pm=0,
 \qquad
 \psi_\pm(t,\br)=e^{\mp\ii\hOmega t}\psi_\pm(0,\br).
\]
Their sum gives the general solution:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\psi(t,\br)=e^{-\ii\hOmega t}\psi_+(0,\br)
                         +e^{\ii\hOmega t}\psi_-(0,\br).}
 \label{eq:kg-general-solution}
\end{equation}
The two initial data determine its complex branch fields by
\[
 \psi_\pm(0,\br)=\tfrac12\left(\psi(0,\br)
             \pm\ii\hOmega^{-1}\partial_t\psi(0,\br)\right).
\]

\subsection{Square roots from Fourier series}
For spatially periodic free KG solutions, write the discrete Fourier series
\[
 \psi=\sum_n c_n f_n,\qquad
 f_n(t,\br)=\exp\!\left(\ii(\omega_n t+2\pi\bk_n\cdot\br)\right).
\]
Here $n$ labels modes, $c_n\in\C$ are constant coefficients,
$\omega_n\in\R$ are signed angular phase frequencies, and $\bk_n\in\R^3$
are wavenumber vectors in cycles per unit length.  For $\bk_n\ne0$,
its real wavelength $\lambda_n>0$ satisfies $\lVert\bk_n\rVert=1/\lambda_n$.
By \eqref{eq:debroglie-momentum}, the mode momentum is $2\pi\bk_n$
in our units.  The $+\omega_n t$ convention gives energy
eigenvalue $-\omega_n$.
Differentiation gives
\begin{align*}
 \Box\psi&=\sum_n(4\pi^2\bk_n^2-\omega_n^2)c_n f_n,\\
 (\Box+m^2)\psi&=\sum_n(4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2-\omega_n^2)c_n f_n.
\end{align*}
Thus each nonzero KG coefficient requires
\[
 \omega_n^2=4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2,\qquad
 \omega_n=\pm\sqrt{4\pi^2\bk_n^2+m^2}.
\]
Both signs are independent modes~\citeat{tong-qft}{pp.~33--34}{page=39}; no common time period is assumed.
A square-root operator acts on the same coefficients by
\[
 \sqrt\Box\,\psi=\sum_n\sqrt{4\pi^2\bk_n^2-\omega_n^2}\,c_n f_n.
\]
Choose one square-root branch consistently.  Squaring restores $\Box$, giving
\begin{equation*}
 \Box+m^2=(m-\ii\sqrt\Box)(m+\ii\sqrt\Box).
\end{equation*}
For the positive spatial root, the same series gives
\begin{align*}
 \hOmega^2\psi&=\sum_n(m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2)c_n f_n,\\
 \hOmega\psi&=\sum_n\sqrt{m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2}\,c_n f_n.
\end{align*}
Thus $e^{\mp\ii\hOmega t}$ multiplies each spatial mode by
$e^{\mp\ii\sqrt{m^2+4\pi^2\bk_n^2}\,t}$.
Unlike $\hOmega$, $\sqrt\Box$ has imaginary eigenvalues on KG modes;
its branch does not select the frequency sign.

\subsection{Electromagnetic coupling}
Recall the real potentials $V,\bA$, charge $q$, and coupled operators
$\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$, $\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$.
The real potential paravector is $\pA=V+\bA\cdot\bsig$, with real
scalar $S=\partial_tV+\pdr\cdot\bA$.
The scalar equation in a field is~\citeat{lenz-quantum}{Eq.~(299)}{page=59}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{(-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2)\psi=0.}
 \label{eq:kg-field}
\end{equation}
The mass-shell equation uses the scalar part $\hEq^2-\hbpq^2$ of
\eqref{eq:covariant-product-plus} or \eqref{eq:covariant-product-minus}.
The product rule gives
\begin{align}
 \hEq^2&=-\partial_t^2-\ii q(\partial_tV)
       -2\ii qV\partial_t+q^2V^2,
 \label{eq:energy-operator-square}\\
 \hbpq^2&=-\pdr^2+\ii q(\pdr\cdot\bA)
       +2\ii q\bA\cdot\pdr+q^2\bA^2.
 \label{eq:momentum-operator-square}
\end{align}
Substituting \eqref{eq:energy-operator-square} and
\eqref{eq:momentum-operator-square} into \eqref{eq:kg-field} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \boxed{\left(\Box+\ii qS-q^2\det \pA+m^2
 +2\ii q(V\partial_t+\bA\cdot\pdr)\right)\psi=0.}
\end{equation*}

All potential derivatives are retained.

\section{The Dirac equation}
\label{sec:dirac-development}

\subsection{Block form and free factorization}

Recall $m>0$ and real space--time coordinates $(t,\br)$.
In the Weyl representation, a Dirac field is the two-entry block
\begin{equation*}
 \bpsi(t,\br)=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpsi_+(t,\br)\\\pvpsi_-(t,\br)\end{Bmatrix}.
\end{equation*}
Each entry takes values in the same fixed two-dimensional complex left ideal
of $\C\oplus\C^3$ from \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
Each has coordinates in $\C^2$, so the block has coordinates in $\C^4$.
On blocks, Hermitian conjugation also transposes entries; sigma
conjugation acts entrywise.  Summing the two local squared norms gives
the nonnegative real density~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(4.97)--(4.99)}{page=105}
\begin{equation}
 \lVert\bpsi\rVert^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi_+^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_+}
 +\scpart{\pvpsi_-^{\mathsf H}\pvpsi_-}.
 \label{eq:dirac-norm}
\end{equation}

For $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$, define the complex-linear block map
\begin{equation}
 \Wop(\pZ):=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\adj {\pZ}\\\pZ&0\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:dirac-block-map}
\end{equation}
In spinor coordinates, $\Wop(\pZ)$ and $\bI$ are matrices in $\C^{4\times4}$.
For differential-operator arguments, $\Wop(\hP)$, $\hD$, and $\hDq$
are instead matrix differential operators on four-component fields.
Here $\bI$ is the two-entry block identity, represented by $\bI_4$.  Applying $\Wop(\pZ)$ twice
multiplies the first entry by $\adj {\pZ}\,\pZ$ and the second by $\pZ\adj {\pZ}$.
Both products are the paravector determinant, so
\begin{equation*}
 \Wop(\pZ)^2=\det(\pZ)\bI.
\end{equation*}
Sigma conjugation on each block entry gives $\Wop(\pZ)^*=\Wop(\pZ^*)$;
Hermitian conjugation also transposes the block:
\begin{equation*}
 \Wop(\pZ)^{\mathsf H}=\Wop(\pZ^*).
\end{equation*}
Thus $\Wop(\pZ)$ is not generally Hermitian.  For operator arguments,
$\operatorname{adj}$ reverses the vector part without reordering derivatives.
For the free field ($q=0$), use the canonical operators in
\eqref{eq:canonical-operators}.  The block map and Dirac operator are
\begin{align}
 \Wop(\hP)&=\begin{Bmatrix}0&\adj\hP\\\hP&0\end{Bmatrix},\notag\\
 \hD(m)&:=\Wop(\hP)-m\bI,
 \label{eq:dirac-operator}\\
 \hD(-m)&=\Wop(\hP)+m\bI. \notag
\end{align}
The Dirac equation is~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.51)}{page=96}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\hD(m)\bpsi=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac}
\end{equation}
Using $\Wop(\hP)^2=(\det\hP)\bI=-\Box\bI$ from
\eqref{eq:free-momentum-product}, multiplication by the opposite-mass operator gives~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(4.53)--(4.54)}{page=96}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 \hD(m)\hD(-m)&=\hD(-m)\hD(m)\\
 &=\Wop(\hP)^2-m^2\bI
 =-(\Box+m^2)\bI.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:dirac-free-factorization}
\end{equation}
Thus every free Dirac solution satisfies the wave form of the mass shell
$\det \pP=E^2-\bp^2=m^2$ in \eqref{eq:mass-shell}:
\begin{equation*}
 (\det\hP-m^2)\bpsi=0
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad (\Box+m^2)\bpsi=0.
\end{equation*}
The original first-order constraint is the coupled pair~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eq.~(4.108)}{page=107}
\begin{align}
 \ii\pvpartial\pvpsi_-&=(\hE-\hbp\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_-=m\pvpsi_+,\label{eq:dirac-free-first}\\
 \ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+&=(\hE+\hbp\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_+=m\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:dirac-free-second}
\end{align}

On one Fourier mode, let $s\in\C$ satisfy $\hD(m)\bpsi=s\bpsi$
for a nonzero amplitude.  Here $\Box$ denotes its scalar mode eigenvalue.
Since $\hD(m)+m\bI=\Wop(\hP)$, \eqref{eq:dirac-free-factorization} gives
\[
 (\hD(m)+m\bI)^2\bpsi=-\Box\bpsi
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)\bpsi=0.
\]
Thus the eigenvalues are
\[
 s=-m\pm\ii\sqrt\Box.
\]
When distinct, each allows two independent complex coefficients in
$\pvpsi_+$, with $\pvpsi_-=\ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+/(s+m)$.
Each root therefore occurs twice, giving the scalar characteristic equation
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bigl((s+m)^2+\Box\bigr)^2=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac-characteristic}
\end{equation}
At $\Box=0$ the roots coalesce with total algebraic multiplicity four.
For KG modes, $\Box=-m^2$, so $s=0,-2m$; the Dirac equation selects $s=0$.
The matrix verification is \eqref{eq:matrix-dirac-characteristic}.

\subsection{Reconstructing free solutions from Klein--Gordon}
Let $\psi':\R^4\to\C$ solve KG and recall the constant basis amplitude
$\pvpsi_+$.  For constants $a,b\in\C$, a two-constant construction is
\begin{equation}
 \bpsi=\frac1m
 \begin{Bmatrix}
  ma+\ii b\pvpartial\\
  \ii a\pvpartial^*+mb
 \end{Bmatrix}\psi'\pvpsi_+.
 \label{eq:kg-dirac-general-lift}
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:kg-dirac-general-lift} into
\eqref{eq:dirac-free-first} and \eqref{eq:dirac-free-second} gives
\begin{align*}
 \ii\pvpartial\pvpsi_--m\pvpsi_+
    &=-\frac{a}{m}(\Box+m^2)\psi'\pvpsi_+=0,\\
 \ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+-m\pvpsi_-
    &=-\frac{b}{m}(\Box+m^2)\psi'\pvpsi_+=0.
\end{align*}
Taking $a=1,b=0$ gives one scalar-seeded solution.  To span every
Dirac field, instead let $\pvpsi$ have the form \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components},
with both scalar coefficients solving KG.  Then
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bpsi=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpsi\\\ii\pvpartial^*\pvpsi/m\end{Bmatrix}.}
 \label{eq:kg-dirac-complete-lift}
\end{equation}
The second Dirac equation holds by construction; the first is KG.
Conversely, every massive free solution has this form with $\pvpsi=\pvpsi_+$.
Equation~\eqref{eq:kg-general-solution} evolves its two coefficients.

\subsection{Potential and paravector components}
Recall the real $\pA=V+\bA\cdot\bsig$, real charge $q$, and
$\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$, $\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$.
The kinetic four-momentum is $\hPq=\hP_0-q\pA$ from
\eqref{eq:mechanical-paravector}.
For the fixed potential $\pA$ and charge $q$, use the interacting
Dirac operator
\begin{align}
 \hDq(m)&:=\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI
 \label{eq:dirac-field-operator}\\
 &=\begin{Bmatrix}
 -m&\adj\hPq\\
 \hPq&-m
 \end{Bmatrix}. \notag
\end{align}
Minimal coupling gives~\citeat{hestenes-spacetime}{Eq.~(191)}{page=32}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\hDq(m)\bpsi=\bigl(\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi=0.}
 \label{eq:dirac-field}
\end{equation}
Its entries are
\begin{align}
 (\hEq-\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_-&=m\pvpsi_+,\label{eq:dirac-entry-one}\\
 (\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_+&=m\pvpsi_-.
 \label{eq:dirac-entry-two}
\end{align}
Isolating the time derivative gives
$\ii\partial_t\bpsi=\hH\bpsi$, where the Hamiltonian is~\citeat{lenz-quantum}{Eq.~(412)}{page=75}
\begin{equation}
 \hH:=qV\bI+\Wop(1)\bigl(m\bI-\Wop(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\bigr).
 \label{eq:dirac-hamiltonian}
\end{equation}
For real potentials, the Dirac equation conserves total probability
when the spatial boundary flux vanishes.  The pointwise density
\eqref{eq:dirac-norm} can still vary through probability flux.

To expose each coefficient, write
$\pvpsi_\pm=a_\pm+\bb_\pm\cdot\bsig$, with complex scalar
functions $a_\pm:\R^4\to\C$ and vector functions
$\bb_\pm:\R^4\to\C^3$. Their pairs $(a_\pm,\bb_\pm)$ are restricted to
the fixed spinor ideal, so their four complex coefficients per entry are
not independent. Sigma multiplication gives
\begin{align*}
 \hEq a_--\hbpq\cdot\bb_-&=ma_+,\\
 \hEq\bb_--\hbpq a_--\ii\hbpq\times\bb_-&=m\bb_+,\\
 \hEq a_++\hbpq\cdot\bb_+&=ma_-,\\
 \hEq\bb_++\hbpq a_++\ii\hbpq\times\bb_+&=m\bb_-.
\end{align*}
Every operator acts on the following coefficient.  Each entry still
has the two independent complex components of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-dirac-components} converts the system to
the conventional four-component Dirac equation.

\subsection{Interacting factorization and reconstruction}
\label{sec:interacting-factorization}

Recall the real potential $\pA=V+\bA\cdot\bsig$, the complex field
$\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\cdot\bsig$, and
$S=\partial_tV+\pdr\cdot\bA$.
For any field $\pvpsi:\R^4\to\C\oplus\C^3$, the product rule gives
\begin{align}
 \pvpartial^*(\pA^*\pvpsi)+\pA\pvpartial\pvpsi
 &=(S+\pF)\pvpsi+2(V\partial_t+\bA\cdot\pdr)\pvpsi,
 \label{eq:potential-product-one}\\
 \pvpartial(\pA\pvpsi)+\pA^*\pvpartial^*\pvpsi
 &=(S+\pF^*)\pvpsi+2(V\partial_t+\bA\cdot\pdr)\pvpsi.
 \label{eq:potential-product-two}
\end{align}
The difference of \eqref{eq:potential-product-one} and
\eqref{eq:potential-product-two} is $(\pF-\pF^*)\pvpsi=2(\bE\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi$.

Applying the block map $\Wop$ twice gives
\begin{align*}
 \Wop(\pvpartial^*)\Wop(\pA)\bpsi
   &=\begin{Bmatrix}\pvpartial(\pA\pvpsi_+)\\\pvpartial^*(\pA^*\pvpsi_-)\end{Bmatrix},\\
 \Wop(\pA)\Wop(\pvpartial^*)\bpsi
   &=\begin{Bmatrix}\pA^*\pvpartial^*\pvpsi_+\\\pA\pvpartial\pvpsi_-\end{Bmatrix},\\
 \Wop(\pA)^2&=\det \pA\,\bI.
\end{align*}
Differentiating a parenthesized product includes the potential derivative.
The argument $-m$ changes only the mass sign, keeping $q$ and $\pA$
fixed.  Multiplying and using the preceding mixed-derivative identities gives
\begin{equation}
 \hDq(-m)\hDq(m)
 =(\hEq^2-\hbpq^2-m^2)\bI
 -\ii q\begin{Bmatrix}\pF^*&0\\0&\pF\end{Bmatrix}.
 \label{eq:dirac-interacting-operator-factorization}
\end{equation}
The derivatives act on potentials as well as amplitudes.
For every Dirac solution,
\[
 \hDq(-m)\hDq(m)\bpsi=0.
\]
At $q=0$, this recovers \eqref{eq:dirac-free-factorization} and hence the
free Klein--Gordon equation. With coupling, its entries obey
\begin{align*}
 \left((\pvpartial+\ii q\pA^*)(\pvpartial^*+\ii q\pA)+m^2\right)\pvpsi_+&=0,\\
 \left((\pvpartial^*+\ii q\pA)(\pvpartial+\ii q\pA^*)+m^2\right)\pvpsi_-&=0.
\end{align*}
Expanding gives the exact second-order equations~\citeat{feynman-gellmann}{Eq.~(6)}{page=2}
\begin{align}
 (-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2+\ii q\pF^*)\pvpsi_+&=0,
 \label{eq:dirac-squared-first}\\
 (-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2+\ii q\pF)\pvpsi_-&=0.
 \label{eq:dirac-squared-field}
\end{align}
For the spin-term components, write $\bZ=\bE+\ii\bBf$ and
$\pvpsi=a+\bb\cdot\bsig$, with complex functions
$a:\R^4\to\C$ and $\bb,\bZ:\R^4\to\C^3$.
Here $\bZ^*$ conjugates its scalar coefficients.  Sigma multiplication gives
\begin{align*}
 \ii q\pF\pvpsi
 &=\ii q\bZ\cdot\bb+
   (\ii qa\bZ-q\bZ\times\bb)\cdot\bsig,
 \\
 \ii q\pF^*\pvpsi
 &=-\ii q\bZ^*\cdot\bb+
   (-\ii qa\bZ^*+q\bZ^*\times\bb)\cdot\bsig.
\end{align*}
To construct solutions, take a complex paravector amplitude $\pvpsi$ of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components} satisfying
\eqref{eq:dirac-squared-field} with $\pvpsi$ in place of $\pvpsi_-$.  Set
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\bpsi=\begin{Bmatrix}(\hEq-\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi/m\\\pvpsi\end{Bmatrix}.}
 \label{eq:dirac-field-reconstruction}
\end{equation}
The first Dirac equation holds by construction; the second is the seed
equation.  Every massive solution has this form with $\pvpsi=\pvpsi_-$.
One must solve that seed equation for the chosen potential: a scalar KG
seed lacks the spin term, and independently solved squared entries need
not satisfy the first-order coupling.

\subsection{Gauge phase}
Recall the real scalar $\lambda:\R^4\to\R$ and gauge transformation
$\pA'=\pA+\pvpartial^*\lambda$, or
$V'=V+\partial_t\lambda$, $\bA'=\bA-\pdr\lambda$.
For $\hEq':=\ii\partial_t-qV'$, $\hbpq':=-\ii\pdr-q\bA'$, and
$\hPq':=\hEq'+\hbpq'\cdot\bsig$, the product rule gives
\begin{align*}
 (\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig)(e^{\ii q\lambda}\pvpsi_\pm')
 &=e^{\ii q\lambda}
   (\hEq-q\partial_t\lambda+\hbpq\cdot\bsig
      +q(\pdr\lambda)\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm'\\
 &=e^{\ii q\lambda}(\hEq'+\hbpq'\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_\pm'.
\end{align*}
The adjugate block follows with the vector signs reversed.
Thus the new amplitude is
\begin{equation*}
 \bpsi'=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\bpsi.
\end{equation*}
Equivalently,
\begin{align*}
 \hEq'\bpsi'&=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\hEq\bpsi,\\
 \hbpq'\bpsi'&=e^{-\ii q\lambda}\hbpq\bpsi.
\end{align*}
Consequently~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(6.68)--(6.69)}{page=143}
\begin{equation}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\bigl(\Wop(\hPq')-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi'\\
 &\qquad=e^{-\ii q\lambda}
       \bigl(\Wop(\hPq)-m\bI\bigr)\bpsi.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:gauge-operator-covariance}
\end{equation}
Equation~\eqref{eq:gauge-operator-covariance} preserves the Dirac equation;
the phase sign is fixed by the potential shift.
The factor $e^{-\ii q\lambda}\in\C$ has modulus one and preserves the
spinor ideal, so the norm is unchanged.  The same product rules
preserve the KG and Pauli equations.

\section{Low-energy limits}
\label{sec:low-energy-limits}

Recall $m\in\R_{>0}$, $q\in\R$, and potentials
$V:\R^4\to\R$, $\bA:\R^4\to\R^3$.
The coupled operators are $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ and
$\hbpq=-\ii\pdr-q\bA$; the fields are
$\bE=-\pdr V-\partial_t\bA$ and $\bBf=\pdr\times\bA$.
Remove the rest oscillation and retain the leading nonrelativistic terms.
This gives Schr\"odinger from KG and Pauli from Dirac.

\subsection{Klein--Gordon to Schr\"odinger}
\label{sec:schrodinger-limit}

The scalar Klein--Gordon equation gives Schr\"odinger dynamics after
removing the rest oscillation. Keep the coupled operators $\hEq,\hbpq$
defined above. For the full scalar KG field $\psi':\R^4\to\C$, define
its scalar envelope $\psi:\R^4\to\C$ by~\citeat{tong-qft}{Eqs.~(2.103)--(2.104)}{page=47}
\begin{equation}
 \psi'(t,\br)=e^{-\ii mt}\psi(t,\br).
 \label{eq:kg-rest-phase}
\end{equation}
The product rule makes the rest-energy shift explicit:
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \hEq\psi'
 &=(\ii\partial_t-qV)(e^{-\ii mt}\psi)\\
 &=e^{-\ii mt}(m\psi+\ii\partial_t\psi-qV\psi)\\
 &=e^{-\ii mt}(m+\hEq)\psi,\\[4pt]
 \hbpq\psi'&=e^{-\ii mt}\hbpq\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
Since $m$ is constant, applying $\hEq$ again gives
$\hEq^2\psi'=e^{-\ii mt}(\hEq+m)^2\psi$; spatial derivatives leave the
phase untouched.  Substitution into \eqref{eq:kg-field} therefore gives
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 0&=(-\hEq^2+\hbpq^2+m^2)\psi'\\
  &=e^{-\ii mt}\bigl(-(\hEq+m)^2+\hbpq^2+m^2\bigr)\psi\\
  &=e^{-\ii mt}(-\hEq^2-2m\hEq+\hbpq^2)\psi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
On the envelope, $\hEq$ measures energy above rest, excluding $qV$.
The rest terms cancel. Divide by the nonzero phase to obtain
\begin{equation}
 \hEq\psi=\frac{\hbpq^2-\hEq^2}{2m}\psi.
 \label{eq:kg-residual-recursion}
\end{equation}
For weak, slowly varying fields and a slow positive-energy envelope,
let $0<v\ll1$ be a real expansion parameter with $\hEq/m=O(v^2)$.
Then $\hEq^2\psi$ is
smaller than $2m\hEq\psi$ by $O(v^2)$.  Dropping it at leading order
gives~\citeat{feynman-schrodinger}{Eq.~(21.3)}{Eq:III:21:3}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ii\partial_t\psi=\left(
 \frac{(-\ii\pdr-q\bA)^2}{2m}+qV\right)\psi.}
 \label{eq:schrodinger}
\end{equation}
With $\bA=0$, hence $\bBf=0$, the kinetic-energy operator is $-\pdr^2/(2m)$,
and the conventional scalar form is
\[
 \ii\partial_t\psi=\left(-\frac{\pdr^2}{2m}+qV\right)\psi.
\]
Schr\"odinger and Pauli occur at the same leading order $1/m$.
The following Dirac limit retains the magnetic spin term; when $\bBf=0$,
each complex coefficient of the Pauli amplitude obeys this same scalar
Schr\"odinger equation.

\subsection{Dirac to Pauli}
\label{sec:low-energy-recursion}
\label{sec:pauli-limit}

The fields $\pvpsi_\pm,\pvpsi_\pm',\pvphi_\pm$ all take values in the same fixed
two-dimensional complex left ideal of $\C\oplus\C^3$, with coordinate
columns in $\C^2$. The phase and change of blocks below preserve this domain.

Apply the same rest-energy phase shift to define the Dirac envelopes~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.1)}{page=4}:
\begin{equation}
 \pvpsi_\pm':=e^{\ii mt}\pvpsi_\pm.
 \label{eq:dirac-rest-phase}
\end{equation}
The product rule gives
\begin{equation*}
 \hEq\pvpsi_\pm=e^{-\ii mt}(m+\hEq)\pvpsi_\pm'.
\end{equation*}
On the envelopes, $\hEq$ measures energy above rest, excluding $qV$.
The two rows of the Dirac equation, \eqref{eq:dirac-entry-one} and
\eqref{eq:dirac-entry-two}, become
\begin{align*}
 (m+\hEq-\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_-'&=m\pvpsi_+',\\
 (m+\hEq+\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi_+'&=m\pvpsi_-'.
\end{align*}
At free positive-energy rest, $\pvpsi_+'=\pvpsi_-'$.  Define the large and small components
\begin{align}
 \pvphi_+&:=\frac{\pvpsi_+'+\pvpsi_-'}{\sqrt2},
 \label{eq:dirac-large-amplitude}\\
 \pvphi_-&:=\frac{\pvpsi_-'-\pvpsi_+'}{\sqrt2}.
 \label{eq:dirac-small-amplitude}
\end{align}
Adding and subtracting gives the exact pair~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eqs.~(2.15)--(2.16)}{page=6}:
\begin{align}
 \hEq\pvphi_+&=(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvphi_-,
 \label{eq:dirac-large-component}\\
 (2m+\hEq)\pvphi_-&=(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvphi_+.
 \label{eq:dirac-small-recursion}
\end{align}
For nonrelativistic positive-energy states in weak, slowly varying fields,
neglect $\hEq\pvphi_-$ relative to $2m\pvphi_-$.  Substitute the small component
into the first equation and use \eqref{eq:kinetic-sigma-square}:
\begin{align*}
 \pvphi_-&\simeq\frac{\hbpq\cdot\bsig}{2m}\pvphi_+,\\
 \hEq\pvphi_+&\simeq\frac{(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)^2}{2m}\pvphi_+
 =\frac{\hbpq^2-q\bBf\cdot\bsig}{2m}\pvphi_+.
\end{align*}
Restoring $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ gives Pauli's equation through order
$1/m$~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.13)}{page=6}:
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ii\partial_t\pvphi_+=\left(
  \frac{(-\ii\pdr-q\bA)^2}{2m}+qV
  -\frac{q}{2m}\bBf\cdot\bsig\right)\pvphi_+.}
 \label{eq:pauli}
\end{equation}
The change of entries preserves the density exactly:
\begin{equation*}
 \lVert\bpsi\rVert^2=\lVert\pvphi_+\rVert^2+\lVert\pvphi_-\rVert^2.
\end{equation*}
The leading probability density is $\lVert\pvphi_+\rVert^2$.
Its two complex components are the Pauli amplitudes; see
Appendix~\ref{sec:matrix-spinors}. This is the amplitude used in the spin discussion.
For a real expansion parameter $0<v\ll1$, the scaling $\hbpq/m=O(v)$, $\hEq/m=O(v^2)$,
$qV/m=O(v^2)$, $q\bBf/m^2=O(v^2)$, $q\bE/m^2=O(v^3)$,
with fields varying on spatial scales $1/(mv)$ and time scales $1/(mv^2)$
or longer, gives $\pvphi_-=O(v\pvphi_+)$ and omitted energy terms of order $mv^4$.

\subsection{Spin components in a uniform magnetic field}
\label{sec:uniform-spin}

For a constant field $\bm B\in\R^3\setminus\{0\}$, define its \hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{unit direction} and \hyperref[sec:algebra-projectors]{spin projectors}:

\[
\bm{u}:=\frac{\bm{B}}{\lVert\bm{B}\rVert}\in\R^3,
\qquad \pvPi_\pm:=\frac12(1\pm\bm{u}\cdot\bsig)\in\R\oplus\R^3.
\]

\hyperref[sec:spectral]{Spectral decomposition} gives

\[
\bm{B}\cdot\bsig
=\lVert\bm{B}\rVert(\pvPi_+-\pvPi_-),
\qquad
\pvPi_\pm(\bm{B}\cdot\bsig)
=\pm\lVert\bm{B}\rVert\pvPi_\pm.
\]

Encode the two spin amplitudes $\phi_{++},\phi_{+-}:\R^4\to\C$ in an aligned paravector
$\pvphi_+:\R^4\to\mathrm{span}_{\C}\{1,\bm u\cdot\bsig\}\subset\C\oplus\C^3$
by spectral decomposition:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvphi_+&=\phi_{++}\pvPi_++\phi_{+-}\pvPi_-,\\
\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+&=\pvphi_+\pvPi_\pm=\phi_{+\pm}\pvPi_\pm.
\end{aligned}
\]

Constant $\pvPi_\pm$ commute with $\hEq$ and $\hbpq^2$,
so the Pauli equation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
0&=\pvPi_\pm\left(2m\hEq-\hbpq^2+q\bm B\cdot\bsig\right)\pvphi_+\\
&=\left(\left(2m\hEq-\hbpq^2
\pm q\lVert\bm B\rVert\right)\phi_{+\pm}\right)\pvPi_\pm.
\end{aligned}
\]

Since $\pvPi_\pm\ne0$, the scalar coefficients vanish.
Restoring $\hEq=\ii\partial_t-qV$ gives
\[
\boxed{\ii\partial_t\phi_{+\pm}=
\left(\frac{\hbpq^2\mp q\lVert\bm B\rVert}{2m}+qV\right)\phi_{+\pm}.}
\]

States with \hyperref[sec:spin-half]{spin projections} $\pm\tfrac12$ along $\bm u$ have opposite
\hyperref[sec:uniform-spin]{Zeeman shifts}. The
\hyperref[eq:mechanical-momentum-operator]{kinetic momentum}
$\hbpq=-\ii\partial_{\bm r}-q\bm A$ retains the orbital coupling.

This representation applies to uniform-field Pauli evolution; the full
Dirac equation and spin measurements use the fixed spinor space.

Arrange the four complex amplitudes of the Dirac spinor, after removing
the rest-energy phase, as:
\[
\bm{\phi}:=\left(\,\begin{matrix}
\phi_{++}&\phi_{+-}\\
\phi_{-+}&\phi_{--}
\end{matrix}\,\right)\in\C^{2\times2}.
\]
Rows label Dirac blocks, columns spin projections; the Pauli limit governs the upper row.

In a basis diagonalizing $\bm u\cdot\bsig$, the aligned paravector is
$\mathrm{diag}(\phi_{++},\phi_{+-})$. The corresponding spinor in the
fixed ideal of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components} is
$\phi_{++}\pZ_++\phi_{+-}\pZ_-$, where constant orthonormal $\pZ_\pm$ satisfy
$\pvPi_\pm \pZ_\pm=\pZ_\pm$ and $\pvPi_\pm \pZ_\mp=0$.
Both representations give the same scalar Pauli equations.
The full Dirac coupling $\hbpq\cdot\bsig$ need not preserve alignment.

For the original spinor blocks, the lower block still satisfies
$\pvphi_-\simeq(\hbpq\cdot\bsig)\pvphi_+/(2m)$.
For a nonzero field with varying direction, its projectors depend on $(t,\br)$.
With $\hH$ the Pauli Hamiltonian in \eqref{eq:pauli},
the product rule for the original spinor gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \ii\partial_t(\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+)
 &=\ii(\partial_t\pvPi_\pm)\pvphi_++\pvPi_\pm \hH\pvphi_+\\
 &=\hH(\pvPi_\pm\pvphi_+)
   +\left(\ii\partial_t\pvPi_\pm
          +\frac{[\pvPi_\pm,\hbpq^2]}{2m}\right)\pvphi_+.
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
Only the projector derivatives and kinetic commutator couple the
sectors; the magnetic term commutes with its own projectors.

\subsection{Evolution about an alternate spin axis}
\label{sec:alternate-spin-alignment}
Let $\bm u'\in\R^3$ be a constant unit vector and $\theta\in[0,\pi]$.
Take initial scalar wavefunctions $\phi_{++},\phi_{+-}:\R^3\to\C$ along this axis:
\[
\begin{gathered}
\bm u'\cdot\bm u=\cos\theta,\qquad
\pvPi_\pm':=\frac12(1\pm\bm u'\cdot\bsig)\in\R\oplus\R^3,\\
\pvphi_+(0)=\phi_{++}\pvPi_+'+\phi_{+-}\pvPi_-'\in\C\oplus\C^3.
\end{gathered}
\]

The magnetic term has both diagonal and off-diagonal parts in these projectors:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvPi_\pm'(\bm u\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\pm'
&=\pm\cos\theta\,\pvPi_\pm',\\
\pvPi_\mp'(\bm u\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\pm'
&=\bigl((\bm u-\cos\theta\,\bm u')\cdot\bsig\bigr)\pvPi_\pm'.
\end{aligned}
\]

Projecting the Pauli equation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
2m\hEq\pvPi_\pm'\pvphi_+
&=\left(\hbpq^2\mp q\lVert\bm B\rVert\cos\theta\right)\pvPi_\pm'\pvphi_+\\
&\quad-q\lVert\bm B\rVert
\pvPi_\pm'(\bm u\cdot\bsig)\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+.
\end{aligned}
\]

Initially $\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+(0)\pvPi_\pm'=0$, but
\[
\left.\ii\partial_t\bigl(\pvPi_\mp'\pvphi_+\pvPi_\pm'\bigr)\right|_{t=0}
=-\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert}{2m}\,
\phi_{+\pm}\bigl((\bm u-\cos\theta\,\bm u')\cdot\bsig\bigr)\pvPi_\pm'.
\]

For $q\ne0$ and $\sin\theta\ne0$, evolution generates off-diagonal components:
two scalar multiples of the fixed $\pvPi_\pm'$ no longer suffice.
At $\theta=0$ the earlier equations return; at $\theta=\pi$ the signs exchange.
For perpendicular axes, the magnetic term is entirely off-diagonal.

For time-independent $V:\R^3\to\R$ and $\bm A:\R^3\to\R^3$,
the orbital operator commutes with the constant magnetic term.
The full Pauli evolution of this initial paravector is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\pvphi_+(t)
&=e^{-\ii t(\hbpq^2/(2m)+qV)}
  e^{\,\ii q\lVert\bm B\rVert t\,\bm u\cdot\bsig/(2m)}\pvphi_+(0)\\
&=e^{-\ii t(\hbpq^2/(2m)+qV)}
  \left(\cos\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert t}{2m}
  +\ii\bm u\cdot\bsig\sin\frac{q\lVert\bm B\rVert t}{2m}\right)\pvphi_+(0).
\end{aligned}
\]
The exponential follows from $(\bm u\cdot\bsig)^2=1$.
Its transverse contribution produces the off-diagonal terms above.

\subsection{The next free order}
\label{sec:free-energy-expansion}

For the free complex scalar envelope $\pvpsi(t,\br)$, set $qV=0$ and
$q\bA=0$, so $\hEq=\hE$, $\hbpq=\hbp$, and the operators commute.
On the positive-frequency KG branch, solving
\eqref{eq:kg-residual-recursion} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \hE\pvpsi=\left(\sqrt{m^2+\hbp^2}-m\right)\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
The same energy acts on either normalized positive-energy Dirac spin
component.  Iteration starts with $\hE\pvpsi\simeq\hbp^2\pvpsi/(2m)$;
its square supplies the next term~\citeat{gurtler-hestenes}{Eq.~(2.19)}{page=6}:
\begin{equation*}
 \hE\pvpsi=\left(\frac{\hbp^2}{2m}-\frac{\hbp^4}{8m^3}
             +O\!\left(\frac{\hbp^6}{m^5}\right)\right)\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}

For the charged scalar equation, the same iteration applies whenever
$\hEq$ commutes with $\hbpq^2$. On the positive-energy branch,
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \hEq\pvpsi
 &=\left(\sqrt{m^2+\hbpq^2}-m\right)\pvpsi\\
 &=\left(\frac{\hbpq^2}{2m}-\frac{\hbpq^4}{8m^3}
   +O\!\left(\frac{\hbpq^6}{m^5}\right)\right)\pvpsi.
 \end{aligned}
\]
This expansion applies when the kinetic momenta in the state are small
compared with $m$.

\section{Spin and probability}
\label{sec:spin-and-probability}
\label{sec:paravector-spin-content}

Here $\pvpsi$ takes values in the fixed spinor ideal of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}, with coordinates in $\C^2$.
Axes and polarizations $\bu,\bu',\ba\in\R^3$ are unit vectors;
angles $\theta,\varphi\in\R$, projectors $\pvPi_\pm,\pvPi_\pm'\in\R\oplus\R^3$,
and probabilities lie in $[0,1]\subset\R$.
The density $\rho\in\R_{\ge0}$ is a probability density.

Spin specifies the intrinsic rotation of an amplitude.  Here we rotate
the state at fixed coordinates.  A scalar KG amplitude stays unchanged
under this intrinsic action and has spin zero; a two-component amplitude
has the one-sided law below.  The wave equation alone does not fix spin:
free Dirac components also satisfy KG.

\subsection{Spin one-half and rotations}
\label{sec:spin-half}

Recall the complex paravector amplitude $\pvpsi(t,\br)$ of
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.  For a
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit axis} $\bu$ and real angle
$\theta$, use the unitary rotor
$\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ of
\eqref{eq:rotation-exponential}.  The spinor law is~\citeat{baylis-spin}{p.~4}{page=4}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi.}
 \label{eq:spin-half-law}
\end{equation}
Writing its infinitesimal action as
$\pvpsi'=\pvpsi-\ii\theta \pS(\bu)\pvpsi+O(\theta^2)$ defines the spin
operator along $\bu$, given by left multiplication by the real pure-vector
paravector $\pS(\bu)\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$~\citeat{sparks-quantum}{p.~62}{page=66}:
\begin{equation}
 \pS(\bu)\pvpsi=\tfrac12(\bu\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi.
 \label{eq:spin-half-generator}
\end{equation}
Since $(\bu\cdot\bsig)^2=1$, its two spin projections are
$\pm1/2$ in units $\hbar=1$.  A $2\pi$ rotation gives $\pR=-1$,
reversing the amplitude's sign; a $4\pi$ rotation restores it.
Probabilities are unchanged by the overall sign, while a relative sign
can be detected by interference~\citeat{feynmanspin}{Eq.~(6.18)}{Eq:III:6:18}.
Each Dirac entry obeys this same active rotation law.

For a real paravector observable $\pZ$, the state law
\eqref{eq:spin-half-law} gives, by \eqref{eq:amplitude-pullback},
\begin{equation*}
 \langle \pZ\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pR^{\mathsf H}\pZ \pR\rangle_\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
The observable inside the original state's pairing rotates through
$-\theta$.  If the observable rotates with the state,
$\pZ'=\pR \pZ \pR^{\mathsf H}$, then
$\langle \pZ'\rangle_{\pvpsi'}=\langle \pZ\rangle_\pvpsi$.

\subsection{Projectors and probabilities}
\label{sec:physical-spinors}

Recall a nonzero Pauli amplitude $\pvpsi$ of \eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}.
For a real unit measurement axis $\bu$, the real projectors are
\begin{equation*}
 \pvPi_\pm=\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig).
\end{equation*}
Their algebra in Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors} gives
\begin{align*}
 \pvpsi&=\pvPi_+\pvpsi+\pvPi_-\pvpsi,\\
 \pS(\bu)(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)&=\pm\tfrac12\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi,\\
 \scpart{(\pvPi_+\pvpsi)^{\mathsf H}(\pvPi_-\pvpsi)}&=0,\\
 \lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
 &=\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi}=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert_{\pvPi_\pm}^2.
\end{align*}
By \eqref{eq:weighted-density}, these are nonnegative weighted densities.
The Born probability is this density divided by
$\rho=\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2$.  In the fixed orthonormal basis
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-basis}, $\pvpsi=c_+\pvpsi_++c_-\pvpsi_-$ with
complex scalars $c_\pm$, so
$\rho=\lVert c_+\rVert^2+\lVert c_-\rVert^2$.
When the measurement axis is that basis axis, the probabilities are
$\lVert c_\pm\rVert^2/\rho$.
The same-side and cross-side projector products are already derived in
Section~\ref{sec:algebra-projectors}.

Define the real spin-polarization vector $\bu'\in\R^3$ by its projection
along every real unit measurement axis $\bu$:
\begin{equation}
 \bu\cdot\bu'
:=\frac{\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}(\bu\cdot\bsig)\pvpsi}}{\rho}.
 \label{eq:spin-state}
\end{equation}
The right side is real and linear in $\bu$, so it uniquely specifies
$\bu'$.  For the arbitrary perpendicular basis axes $\bu,\ba$ in
\eqref{eq:axis-ideal-components}, expanding the same pairing gives
\begin{equation*}
 \begin{aligned}
 \rho\bu'={}&(\lVert c_+\rVert^2-\lVert c_-\rVert^2)\bu\\
 &+2\repart{c_+^*c_-}\ba
 +2\impart{c_+^*c_-}(\bu\times\ba).
 \end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
These three directions are orthonormal, hence
\begin{equation*}
 \rho^2(\bu')^2
 =(\lVert c_+\rVert^2-\lVert c_-\rVert^2)^2+4\lVert c_+^*c_-\rVert^2
 =\rho^2.
\end{equation*}
Thus $\bu'$ is a real unit vector, with mean spin $\bu'/2$.
For any real unit measurement axis,
inserting $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm\bu\cdot\bsig)/2$ directly into the pairing gives
\[
 \lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
 =\scpart{\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi}
 =\tfrac12\rho(1\pm\bu\cdot\bu').
\]
Therefore~\citeat{baylis-spin}{Eqs.~(66)--(67)}{page=12}
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\mathrm{prob}(\pm\mid\bu)=\frac{\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2}{\rho}
                    =\tfrac12(1\pm\bu\cdot\bu').}
 \label{eq:spin-probability-intrinsic}
\end{equation}
The mean spin along $\bu$ is
$(\mathrm{prob}(+)-\mathrm{prob}(-))/2=\bu\cdot\bu'/2$.
For a spatial wavefunction, \eqref{eq:spin-probability-intrinsic}
is the spin probability conditional on position, not a position density.

\subsection{Measurement along a second axis}
\label{sec:spin-measurement-angle}

Use $\bu'$ for the prepared spin direction and $\bu$ for the measurement
axis, both real unit vectors. Prepare a nonzero spin-up amplitude
$\pvpsi=\pvPi_+'\pvpsi$, where $\pvPi_+'=(1+\bu'\cdot\bsig)/2$.
Let $0\le\theta\le\pi$ be the angle between the axes and write
\[
 K':=\bu'\cdot\bsig,\qquad K:=\bu\cdot\bsig,\qquad
 c':=\bu\cdot\bu'=\cos\theta.
\]

Here $K,K'\in\{0\}\oplus\R^3$ and $c'\in[-1,1]\subset\R$.
The Pauli product gives $(K')^2=1$ and $K'K+KK'=2c'$.
Multiplication by $K'$ gives $K'KK'=2c'K'-K$, hence
\[
 \begin{aligned}
 \pvPi_+'K\pvPi_+'
 &=\frac14(1+K')K(1+K')\\
 &=\frac14(K+K'K+KK'+K'KK')\\
 &=\frac14(2c'+2c'K')=c'\pvPi_+'.
 \end{aligned}
\]
The measurement projectors $\pvPi_\pm=(1\pm K)/2$ therefore satisfy
\begin{equation}
 \pvPi_+'\pvPi_\pm\pvPi_+'
 =\frac12\left(\pvPi_+'\pm\pvPi_+'K\pvPi_+'\right)
 =\frac{1\pm c'}{2}\pvPi_+'.
 \label{eq:spin-projector-overlap}
\end{equation}

Using $\lVert \pZ\rVert^2=\scpart{\pZ \pZ^{\mathsf H}}$ for $\pZ\in\C\oplus\C^3$,
the denominator is
\[
\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2=\scpart{\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}>0.
\]

For the prepared state, the projected squared norm is
\[
\begin{aligned}
\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2
&=\scpart{(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)(\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi)^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\scpart{\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\scpart{\pvPi_+'\pvPi_\pm\pvPi_+'\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}\\
&=\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2}\scpart{\pvpsi\pvpsi^{\mathsf H}}
=\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2}\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2.
\end{aligned}
\]

The Born rule divides by the nonzero squared norm, so no prior normalization
is needed:
\begin{equation}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathrm{prob}(\pm)
 :=\frac{\lVert\pvPi_\pm\pvpsi\rVert^2}{\lVert\pvpsi\rVert^2}
 =\frac{1\pm\cos\theta}{2},\\[4pt]
 \boxed{\mathrm{prob}(+)=\cos^2\frac\theta2,\qquad
 \mathrm{prob}(-)=\sin^2\frac\theta2.}
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:spin-half-angle-probabilities}
\end{equation}
Here $+$ and $-$ label measured spin projections $+1/2$ and $-1/2$ along $\bu$.
The probabilities sum to one. Aligned axes give spin up with certainty,
perpendicular axes give equal probabilities, and opposite axes give
spin down with certainty. Preparing $\pvpsi=\pvPi_-'\pvpsi$ interchanges the
probabilities. For a spatial wavefunction with the same spin preparation
at every position, integrating both norm densities gives the same result.

\subsection{Rotating the polarization}

For $\pR=\exp(-\ii\varphi\ba\cdot\bsig/2)$, with real unit rotation
axis $\ba$ and real angle $\varphi$, let $\bu''$ denote the polarization
of the rotated amplitude $\pvpsi'=\pR\pvpsi$. The amplitude law
\eqref{eq:spin-half-law} gives
\begin{equation*}
 \bu\cdot\bu''=\langle \pR^{\mathsf H}(\bu\cdot\bsig)\pR\rangle_\pvpsi.
\end{equation*}
This is the inverse of Rodrigues' rotation~\eqref{eq:rodrigues}:
the measurement axis rotates through $-\varphi$, so $\bu''$ rotates
through $+\varphi$ about $\ba$.
Thus the polarization rotates as a vector although its amplitude has
spin one-half.  Its overlap with the original polarization is
\[
 \bu'\cdot\bu''=(\ba\cdot\bu')^2
   +\left(1-(\ba\cdot\bu')^2\right)\cos\varphi.
\]
For $\ba\cdot\bu'=0$, the probability to retain the initial spin
is $\cos^2(\varphi/2)$.

\section{Lorentz covariance and electromagnetic transformations}
\label{sec:transformations}

At corresponding events, the domains are
\[
 \begin{gathered}
 \pX,\pU,\pP,\pA,\pJ\in\R\oplus\R^3,\qquad
 \pF\in\{0\}\oplus\C^3,\\
 \pT\in\C\oplus\C^3,\quad \det \pT=1,\qquad
 \pR\in\R\oplus\ii\R^3,\quad \pL\in\R\oplus\R^3,\\
 t,s,E,V,\rho,\theta,\beta,\gamma\in\R,\qquad
 \br,\bv,\bp,\bA,\bE,\bBf,\bJ,\bu\in\R^3.
 \end{gathered}
\]
Here $\bu^2=1$, $|\beta|<1$, $\gamma\ge1$, and $\rho$ is charge density.
Transformed quantities retain their types. Scalar KG fields take values in
$\C$; Dirac entries remain in their fixed two-dimensional complex spinor ideal.

We now apply the algebraic actions of Section~\ref{sec:preserved-quantities}
to the physical variables already defined.  Appendix~\ref{sec:conventions}
compares alternative exponent signs and sandwich orders.  Primed fields are evaluated at
$\pX'$ and unprimed fields at $\pX$.  The frame factors here are constant;
varying factors are treated in Section~\ref{sec:varying-transformations}.

\subsection{Transformation laws for fields and derivatives}

Let $\pT$ be a complex \hyperref[eq:unit-paravector]{invertible paravector}
with \hyperref[eq:unit-determinant-paravector]{$\det \pT=1$}.  It may be any ordered
product of $\pR=\exp(-\ii\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$ and
$\pL=\exp(-\theta\bu\cdot\bsig/2)$, with
\hyperref[def:real-unit-vector]{real unit vector} $\bu$ and real angle
or rapidity $\theta$.  Recall the real event $\pX$, momentum $\pP$, potential
$\pA$, and electric current $\pJ$.
Each transforms by~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(24)}{page=20}
\begin{equation}
 \pZ'=\pT \pZ \pT^{\mathsf H},
 \qquad \pZ=\pX,\pP,\pA,\pJ.
 \label{eq:physical-lorentz-congruence}
\end{equation}
Since $\det \pT=1$, sigma conjugation gives $\pT^*=(\pT^{-1})^{\mathsf H}$.
The derivative and complex field $\pF=(\bE+\ii\bBf)\cdot\bsig$ use~\citeat{baylis-intro}{Eq.~(33)}{page=22}
\begin{align}
 \pvpartial'&=\pT^*\pvpartial \pT^{-1},
 \label{eq:derivative-transformation}\\
 \pF'&=\pT \pF \pT^{-1}.
 \label{eq:field-transformation}
\end{align}
Equation~\eqref{eq:derivative-transformation} follows from the chain rule.
For a differentiable scalar $f:\R^4\to\C$ with $f'(\pX')=f(\pX)$,
\[
 \dd f=\scpart{(\pvpartial f)\dd \pX}
 =\scpart{\bigl(\pT^*(\pvpartial f)\pT^{-1}\bigr)\dd \pX'}.
\]
Since this holds for every real increment $\dd \pX'$, the coefficient is
$\pvpartial'f'$~\citeat{tong-em}{p.~101}{page=109}.

\subsection{Maxwell covariance}

The field law follows from the potential law.
The identity~\eqref{eq:potential-field}, with real scalar
$S=\scpart{\pvpartial \pA}$, gives
\[
 \pvpartial'\pA'=\pT^*(S+\pF^*)\pT^{\mathsf H}.
\]
Scalar-part cyclicity gives $S'=S$.  Since $\pT^*=(\pT^{\mathsf H})^{-1}$,
sigma conjugation of the vector part gives
\eqref{eq:field-transformation}.  Maxwell covariance now follows by cancellation:
\begin{equation*}
 \pvpartial'\pF'=\pT^*(\pvpartial \pF)\pT^{-1}
 =\pT^*\pJ^*\pT^{-1}=\pJ'^*.
\end{equation*}
